```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0003
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 257a-257b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  start_marker: 257a
  end_marker: 257b
  start_char: 10
  end_char: 779
  text_sha256: 858776b7eb4e2eda416ec7fa5479154d4fb656e2121b1b0a4c7d73a5abd68d7d
greek_terms:
  - κατὰ τὴν ἀναλογίαν τὴν τῆς ὑμετέρας τέχνης
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates playfully rebukes Theodorus at 257a-257b for ranking the sophist, statesman, and philosopher equally in value, arguing that in actual honor they differ more from one another than the proportion (analogia) recognized by Theodorus's own craft of mathematics would permit.
textual_basis: At 257a-257b Socrates says Theodorus has "assigned each of the men equal worth (tes ises axias), though they stand apart from one another in honor (time) by more than the proportion (analogian) of your craft (technes)." Theodorus concedes the rebuke at 257b.
limits: This observation does not evaluate whether Socrates's ranking of the three figures is correct or ironic in a deeper sense. It notes only the explicit use of mathematical analogy to deliver a gentle reproach.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - μεταλαβόντες
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger proposes to relieve the exhausted Theaetetus by substituting Socrates the Younger as interlocutor, explicitly framing this as a rest for Theaetetus and a continuation of the gymnastic exercise.
textual_basis: "At 257c, after Theodorus prompts the Stranger to choose either the philosopher or the statesman type first, the Stranger responds that they must not abandon the inquiry, then asks what to do about Theaetetus: 'let us give him a rest by taking in exchange his fellow-gymnast, this Socrates.' Theodorus endorses the substitution at 257c."
limits: Does not prove that the substitution alters the inquiry's method or that the previous dialogue (Theaetetus) is formally referenced. The observation records only the explicit procedural move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 258b the Eleatic Stranger announces that after the Sophist they must now investigate the statesman and asks the young Socrates whether this figure should be counted among those possessing knowledge.
textual_basis: The Stranger says it is necessary to investigate the statesman and immediately poses the classificatory question whether this man too must be placed among the knowers. This marks the definiendum and proposes a classification procedure.
limits: This records only that the inquiry's target is named and that a classification approach is proposed. It does not assess whether the classification succeeds or what definition of the statesman follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 258a Socrates reports that he conversed with Theaetetus yesterday and has now heard him answer, while the young Socrates has done neither, so the young Socrates should now answer. The Eleatic Stranger then addresses the young Socrates by name and secures his consent to the procedure.
textual_basis: "Socrates says he conversed with Theaetetus yesterday through speeches and has now heard him answering, but has had neither experience with young Socrates; therefore young Socrates too must be examined. The Stranger then addresses the young Socrates by name and the young Socrates assents. This establishes the dramatic cast: Socrates, young Socrates, Theodorus (mentioned by context), and the Eleatic Stranger."
limits: This records only the dramatic introduction and role assignment of the young Socrates as respondent. It does not analyze the dramatic significance of the substitution of young Socrates for Theaetetus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0010
source_work: Statesman
greek_terms:
  - ἰδέαν αὐτῇ μίαν ἐπισφραγίσασθαι
  - εἶδος ἐπισημηναμένους
english_gloss: seal a single idea upon it and mark off one separate form for the other paths
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 258c-258d
  start_marker: 258c
  end_marker: 258d
  start_char: 2261
  end_char: 2992
  text_sha256: 00aa7991fc5aec684927481a94528317561d7f548182ed76a90ef44ac7cc586d
stephanus_span: 258c-258d
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger states a methodological requirement that the statesman's path must be found, separated from others, sealed with a single idea, and the other paths marked off as one distinct form so that all kinds of knowledge are conceived as two forms.
textual_basis: |
  At 258c the Stranger says one must find the political path, separate it, and "seal a single idea upon it" (idean autei mian episphragisasthai), then "mark off one other form for the other turnings" (tais allais ektropais hen allo eidos episēmēnamenous), and cause the soul to think all sciences as being two forms (duo eidē).
limits: |
  The passage announces a methodological intention; it does not complete the division or produce the two forms here. The vocabulary of idea and eidos is deployed but not defined.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0011
source_work: Statesman
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: this is now your work, I think, not mine
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 258c-258d
  start_marker: 258c
  end_marker: 258d
  start_char: 2261
  end_char: 2992
  text_sha256: 00aa7991fc5aec684927481a94528317561d7f548182ed76a90ef44ac7cc586d
stephanus_span: 258c-258d
observation: |
  Young Socrates resists accepting the inquiry as his own task, attributing it to the Stranger, and the Stranger insists it must belong to Socrates as well once it becomes clear.
textual_basis: |
  At 258c Young Socrates says "this is now your task, I think, stranger, not mine" (tout' ēdē son oimai to ergon). At 258d the Stranger replies "it must, Socrates, be yours too whenever it becomes manifest to us" (dei ge mēn … auto einai kai son). The exchange negotiates who bears responsibility for the inquiry.
limits: |
  This is a procedural negotiation between two speakers, not a full statement of method. The agreement to share responsibility for the inquiry is stated but not yet acted on.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger divides knowledge by its relation to action, distinguishing knowledge-only arts from arts whose knowledge is joined to making, and then naming the two branches as practical and cognitive.
textual_basis: The passage contrasts arithmetic and related arts with carpentry and handwork, then directs all knowledge to be divided into the two named branches.
limits: This does not include the preceding one-form methodological program or the following question about whether political, royal, masterly, and household management names mark one art or many.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: statesman, king, master, household-manager
observation: |
  The Stranger asks whether statesman, king, master, and household-manager should be treated as one thing under multiple names or as distinct crafts, then invites Young Socrates to follow a path to decide.
textual_basis: |
  At 258e-259a the Stranger poses the question whether to treat the statesman, king, master, and household-manager as one thing with many names or as as many distinct crafts as there are names spoken, and says "rather follow me here."
limits: |
  The observation records the contrast between name plurality and thing-unity raised procedurally; it does not adjudicate whether all four are ultimately one craft.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0015
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 258e-259a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 258e-259a
  start_marker: 258e
  end_marker: 259a
  start_char: 2992
  end_char: 3832
  text_sha256: df621c15057271f70d5c70ce01e425fd26ad430174f26c5de466144a54765425
greek_terms:
  - ἰδιώτης
  - ἰδιωτεύων
english_gloss: private person
observation: |
  The Stranger uses a private adviser analogy to argue that the name of a craft belongs to anyone who can give competent advice in that domain, even as a private person.
  He extends the analogy from public doctors to the kingly craft: a private person skilled in advising a king is said to have the knowledge the ruler himself ought to possess.
textual_basis: |
  At 259a the Stranger asks: if a private person is capable of advising public doctors, must he not be called by the same craft-name as the one he advises? Then he immediately applies the same structure to a private person advising a ruling king.
limits: |
  The observation records the analogy structure; it does not evaluate whether the Stranger ultimately endorses this as a sufficient criterion for craft-identity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0016
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 259b-259c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 259b-259c
  start_marker: 259b
  end_marker: 259c
  start_char: 3832
  end_char: 4642
  text_sha256: 5d6803731a4e22f8b656e798ece79b168d77728c279b9a9654658a7fd31c284c
greek_terms:
  - οἰκονόμος
  - δεσπότης
  - μεγάλης σχῆμα οἰκήσεως
  - σμικρᾶς αὖ πόλεως ὄγκος
  - ἀρχὴν
english_gloss: household manager, master, large household, small city, rule
observation: The Stranger asserts equivalence between household manager (oikonomos) and master (despotes), then claims a large household and a small city differ not at all with respect to rule (arche). This collapses the scale distinction between domestic and political domains.
textual_basis: At 259b the Stranger states oikonomos and despotes are the same (tauton). He then asks whether a large household and a small city differ with respect to rule, and Young Socrates answers not at all (ouden). This sets up the unification at 259c.
limits: The observation records the collapse of scale as a move within the definitional argument. It does not assess whether the argument succeeds or what further implications follow.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0017

greek_terms:
  - οἰκονομικήν
english_gloss: one knowledge concerning all these, kingly, political, economic
observation: The Stranger unifies three distinct name-domains — kingly (basilike), political (politike), and economic (oikonomike) — under a single knowledge (episteme mia), and declares that terminological disputes about which name to use should not matter.
textual_basis: At 259c the Stranger says it is now clear that there is one knowledge concerning all these things (episteme mia peri pant' esti tauta) and adds that we should differ not at all whether someone names it kingly or political or economic.
limits: This records the unification claim as a definitional move. It does not assess whether the unification holds under scrutiny or how it relates to later distinctions in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - γνωστικῆς
  - χειροτεχνικῆς
  - ψυχῆς σύνεσιν καὶ ῥώμην
english_gloss: cognitive, handicraft, understanding and strength of soul
observation: The Stranger classifies the kingly art as belonging to the cognitive kind (gnostike) rather than the manual or handicraft kind (cheirotechnike), arguing that a king can accomplish little with hands and body compared to understanding and strength of soul for holding rule.
textual_basis: At 259c the Stranger states that any king can accomplish only small things with hands and whole body for holding rule compared to the understanding and strength of the soul (pros ten tes psyches synesin kai rhomen). He concludes the king belongs more to the cognitive than the handicraft kind.
limits: This records the cognitive/manual distinction as a craft-classification move. It does not evaluate whether the dichotomy holds or how it relates to the method of division being developed in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0019
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260a-260b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260a-260b
  start_marker: 260a
  end_marker: 260b
  start_char: 5421
  end_char: 6230
  text_sha256: 7a58685f4372ff03551c879aa0f2827d57186cd2628c57f281f67dc2f26247f4
greek_terms:
  - γνωστικῆς
  - ἐπιτακτικὸν
  - κριτικὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger proposes dividing all cognitive knowledge (sympases tes gnostikes) into two kinds (gene): the commanding (epitaktikon) part and the critical (kritikon) part, distinguished by whether the knower merely judges or also issues orders after judging. Young Socrates assents to the division."
textual_basis: At 260a the Stranger distinguishes the two kinds by judgment (krisei) and command (epitaxei) as the basis of their difference. At 260b he explicitly frames this as a division of the whole of cognitive knowledge into commanding and critical parts and asks whether the division is well made, which Young Socrates affirms.
limits: This observation records the dichotomous division procedure within the cognitive branch of knowledge; it does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or whether the two branches are coordinate. The observation does not address earlier divisions in the dialogue (e.g., cognitive vs. practical knowledge).
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0020
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260a-260b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260a-260b
  start_marker: 260a
  end_marker: 260b
  start_char: 5421
  end_char: 6230
  text_sha256: 7a58685f4372ff03551c879aa0f2827d57186cd2628c57f281f67dc2f26247f4
greek_terms:
  - λογιστὴς
  - κρίναντι
  - προστάττειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger uses the calculator (logistes) as a contrast case to distinguish the two branches of cognitive knowledge. The calculator's function ends with judgment — he is released (apellachthai) after that — whereas the commanding kind must go further and assign to each worker what is fitting until the ordered task is completed.
textual_basis: At 260a the Stranger says that to the commanding kind it belongs, having judged (krinanti), not to be finished or released as the calculator was released, but to assign (prostattein) to each of the workers the fitting task until they complete what was ordered. The calculator craft is invoked solely to mark the boundary where the critical function ends and the commanding function continues beyond it.
limits: This observation records the calculator as a contrast case within a diairetic division; it does not claim the calculator is being defined or that the passage offers a general theory of craft knowledge. The observation does not assess whether the calculator analogy appears elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0021
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260a-260b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260a-260b
  start_marker: 260a
  end_marker: 260b

  end_char: 6230
  text_sha256: 7a58685f4372ff03551c879aa0f2827d57186cd2628c57f281f67dc2f26247f4
greek_terms:
  - κοινῇ τι πράττουσιν
  - ἀγαπητὸν ὁμονοεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After Young Socrates assents to the division of cognitive knowledge, the Stranger explicitly marks their shared inquiry procedure: for those doing something in common (koinei ti prattousin), it is welcome to agree (agapeton homonoein), and as long as they share in the inquiry, they should dismiss the opinions of others."
textual_basis: At 260b, immediately after Young Socrates assents to the division being well made, the Stranger says that for those acting in common, agreement is welcome, and that while they themselves share in the inquiry (koinonomen), the opinions of others should be dismissed. This marks a procedural moment where the interlocutors reaffirm their commitment to the shared diairetic method over external judgments.
limits: This observation records a procedural agreement marker within the diairetic inquiry; it does not claim the Stranger is closing off criticism or asserting dogmatic authority. The observation does not compare this procedural move with similar markers elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  []

observation: At 259d, the Eleatic Stranger proposes that politikos (political), politikē (political art), basilikos (kingly), and basilikē (kingly art) should all be set down into the same as one (eis tauton hōs hen). The young Socrates immediately assents without qualification.
textual_basis: "The Stranger explicitly states at 259d: tēn ara politikēn kai politikon kai basilikēn kai basilikon eis tauton hōs hen panta tauta synthēsomen. The move conflates four distinct terms into a single referent and subordinates the distinction between king and statesman to a unified search object."
limits: This observation records the syncretism of these terms at 259d. It does not assess whether the identification holds across the dialogue, nor does it claim that the conflation is later revised or challenged.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0023

  text_sha256: feeb6c505267ce36ca66a80170671900068abb69f0c6335bec4cd9a904f7d3b2
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 259e, the Stranger introduces the architect (architektōn) as a paradigm for the kingly/political art: the architect does not do manual labor himself but rules over workmen by supplying knowledge (gnōsis) rather than handiwork (cheirourgia)."
textual_basis: "The Stranger states at 259e: architektōn ge pas ouk autos ergatikos all' ergatōn archōn, and then glosses this as providing gnōsis rather than cheirourgia. Young Socrates assents after each step. This immediately follows the logical illustration that arithmetic merely judges differences in number without doing further work."
limits: This observation records the introduction of the architect analogy as a single craft paradigm for the statesman's function. It does not evaluate whether the analogy holds up beyond this passage, nor does it treat the architect as the sole or dominant paradigm in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Young Socrates responds to the Stranger's setup with a sequence of short assents and prompts while the Stranger equates the political and kingly arts and introduces arithmetic and architecture as cognitive-art examples.
textual_basis: At 259d-259e, Young Socrates gives brief affirmative replies to the identification of kingly and political art, to the proposal to divide cognitive art, and to the arithmetic and architecture examples. The replies keep the Stranger's division moving without independent objection.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0025
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260c-260d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260c-260d
  start_marker: 260c
  end_marker: 260d
  start_char: 6230
  end_char: 6991
  text_sha256: b2479003445f6c352c4172f214da13d5e5f0f8e5bdd7198765e39d60756d6b72
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Stranger locates the kingly art by successive division: he first asks whether it belongs to the critical (spectator-like) or directive (master-like) art (260c), then proposes to examine whether the directive art itself is divided, distinguishing the kingly kind from the heraldic kind through an analogy (260c-260d).
textual_basis: |
  The Stranger explicitly poses the taxonomy question at 260c and immediately subdivides the directive art at 260c-260d. The movement from a binary choice to a further subdivision of the chosen branch is the signature of diairetic method.
limits: |
  This observation records only the structural division step within 260c-260d. It does not claim that a complete diairesis is achieved here; the Stranger has not yet reached a terminal definition of the statesman.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0026
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260c-260d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260c-260d
  start_marker: 260c
  end_marker: 260d
  start_char: 6230
  end_char: 6991
  text_sha256: b2479003445f6c352c4172f214da13d5e5f0f8e5bdd7198765e39d60756d6b72
greek_terms:
  - κάπηλοι
  - κηρύκων
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Stranger distinguishes the kingly kind from the heraldic kind by analogy to the distinction between retailers and self-sellers: just as retailers resell goods already sold by others, heralds reissue commands already formulated by others (260c-260d).
textual_basis: |
  The Stranger explicitly constructs the analogy at 260c-260d: just as the art of retailers is distinguished from the art of self-sellers, so too the kingly kind appears to be distinguished from the heraldic kind. He then unpacks both halves: retailers receive others' works and resell them (260d), and heralds receive others' thoughts and reissue them as commands (260d).
limits: |
  The analogy functions within the diairetic division of the directive art. It does not itself establish what the kingly art positively is; it only separates it from the merely heraldic or interpretive function.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0027
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260c-260d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260c-260d
  start_marker: 260c
  end_marker: 260d
  start_char: 6230
  end_char: 6991
  text_sha256: b2479003445f6c352c4172f214da13d5e5f0f8e5bdd7198765e39d60756d6b72
greek_terms:
  - πῶς γὰρ οὐ μᾶλλον
  - πάνυ μὲν οὖν
  - ἀληθέστατα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Young Socrates issues four consecutive replies within 260c-260d, all brief and affirmative: a rhetorical agreement at 260c, a one-word prompt for explanation at 260d, a strong confirmation at 260d, and a superlative endorsement at 260d.
textual_basis: |
  The four replies are all short. The first is a rhetorical assent (260c), the second a single-word question (260d), the third a strong confirmation particle (260d), and the fourth a superlative affirmative (260d). None exceeds a few words, and all accept or prompt the Stranger's account without resistance or independent contribution.
limits: |
  This pattern of short assent is characteristic of the respondent role in many Platonic dialogues. It does not by itself indicate whether Young Socrates fully understands the division or merely follows the Stranger's lead.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0028
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260e-261a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  text_sha256: c192865bb13bef3b84e853ddc0749eaab91effbb9f1f8ee9c13001fc2b809e67
greek_terms:
  - αὐτεπιτακτῶν
  - αὐτεπιτακτικὴν
english_gloss: self-ordering
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger coins a new term for the class of "self-orderers" to name the genus of the king, distinguishing it from subordinate command-arts like the pilot's, seer's, and herald's arts. He acknowledges that the class is nearly nameless and proposes the coinage as a placeholder, leaving it to others to supply a better name later.
textual_basis: |
  At 260e the Stranger lists command-arts that issue orders, then at 260e-261a introduces the nearly-nameless class of self-orderers, placing the king's kind into the self-ordering art and dismissing the rest as irrelevant to the inquiry about the ruler.
limits: |
  The observation records the coinage of a classificatory term, not whether the term is adequate or retained later in the dialogue. It does not claim this is a definition of the statesman.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἄρχοντος
  - ἐναντίου
  - ἀμελήσαντες
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger narrows the inquiry by setting aside the other command-arts and saying that the method was for the sake of the ruler, not the opposite.
textual_basis: At 260e-261a, after listing command-related arts and naming the self-ordering kind, the Stranger places the king's kind there, dismisses the remaining arts for others to name, and states that the inquiry is for the ruler rather than the opposite.
limits: This records an explicit scope delimitation. It does not establish what the opposite of the ruler is or whether the exclusion remains adequate later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0030
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 260e-261a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 260e-261a
  start_marker: 260e
  end_marker: 261a
  start_char: 6991
  end_char: 7817
  text_sha256: c192865bb13bef3b84e853ddc0749eaab91effbb9f1f8ee9c13001fc2b809e67
greek_terms:
  - ἀλλοτριότητι
  - οἰκειότητα
  - τομὴν
english_gloss: alienness / kinship / cut
observation: |
  At 261a the Stranger frames the next division using the language of alienness and kinship: the self-ordering kind has been separated from the subordinate arts by alienness in relation to kinship. He then asks whether a further cut exists within this kind.
textual_basis: |
  At 261a the Stranger says that since the self-ordering kind has been moderately separated from those others, distinguished by alienness toward kinship, it is necessary to divide this again if there is still a cut yielding within it. The language thematizes the criteria of the diairetic procedure itself.
limits: |
  The observation identifies the explicit procedural vocabulary. It does not assess whether the division is correct or exhaustive, nor does it track whether the alienness/kinship vocabulary recurs.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0031
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 261b-261c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 261b-261c
  start_marker: 261b
  end_marker: 261c
  start_char: 7817
  end_char: 8659
  text_sha256: 0284f352cf67851f7e363526aa0327599277edb74ae4cefd983f410f4ea5350a
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger continues the diairetic method by proposing to divide all generated things in two (dicha) at 261b, then applies the dichotomy to the commanding part of the cognitive kind, dividing it into that over inanimate genesis and that over animate genesis at 261b-261c, and declares that the whole will now be divided in two.
textual_basis: At 261b the Stranger says all generated things can be taken in two (dicha dialabein). At 261c he orders one part under inanimate genesis and the other under animate genesis, concluding that the whole is now divided in two (pan houtos ede diairesetai dicha).
limits: This observation records the continuation of the diairetic method already underway; it does not assess whether this particular dichotomy is valid or whether it exhausts the domain.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0032
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 261b-261c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  start_marker: 261b
  end_marker: 261c

  end_char: 8659
  text_sha256: 0284f352cf67851f7e363526aa0327599277edb74ae4cefd983f410f4ea5350a
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 261c the Stranger explicitly sets aside one branch of the dichotomy (the commanding part over inanimate things) and takes up the other (the commanding part over living beings) for further division.
textual_basis: The Stranger says at 261c to leave aside one branch (to men toinun auton paralipomen) and take up the other (to d' analabomen), then divide what is taken up into two (analabontes de merisometha eis duo to sumpan).
limits: This records the procedural move of deferring a branch; it does not assess whether the deferred branch will later be revisited or whether the choice of which branch to pursue is justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0033

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates the Younger responds to the Stranger's diairetic proposals throughout 261b-261c with minimal assents and brief clarifying questions, never challenging or elaborating on the divisions.
textual_basis: 'His responses are: "How not?" (261b), "In what way?" (261b), "Yes" (261b), "In virtue of what?" (261b), "Absolutely" (261c), and "Which of the two do you mean we should take up?" (261c). None exceeds a few words.'
limits: This records the turn pattern; it does not claim that Socrates the Younger understands the argument or that his assent is coerced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0034
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 262c-262d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 262c-262d
  start_marker: 262c
  end_marker: 262d
  start_char: 10211
  end_char: 11020
  text_sha256: 83cd455712e25cdeef2e1a6a5622b17c0b227b260dc5c1df4cd6bd7bb22db15f
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger identifies a specific methodological error in the preceding diairetic procedure: they did not divide correctly. He promises to clarify the error for the sake of young Socrates' nature (eunoia tes ses physeos) and acknowledges the difficulty of a fully adequate explanation now, but resolves to advance the exposition a little further for clarity's sake."
textual_basis: Young Socrates asks what error in dividing he is referring to (262c). The Stranger frames the correction as an act of goodwill toward young Socrates' nature and signals that the current exposition will be incomplete but that he will push it a little further (smikroi pleon) for clarity (262c).
limits: This observation registers the meta-methodological move of identifying a division error and the pedagogical framing. It does not specify what the error is; the error itself is described at 262c-262d.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: The Stranger illustrates a faulty division by describing a split that isolates the Hellenic as one part and treats all other peoples as one kind because they receive one shared name.
textual_basis: The example separates one named group from all others, then notes that the remaining groups are many and mutually discordant despite being gathered under a single designation.
limits: This record covers only the human-group example. It does not include the number example or the later preferred alternatives.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The Stranger gives a second faulty division, cutting off a myriad from all numbers and treating the remainder as one kind, then contrasts that with dividing number by even and odd and humans by male and female.
textual_basis: The passage first describes the one-sided number cut and the naming of the remainder, then states two more orderly alternatives for number and human classification.
limits: This records the illustrative method contrast. It does not decide how the correction applies to every later division in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0036
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 262c-262d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Young Socrates asks the Stranger to specify what error they made in dividing: he asks what kind of thing the Stranger means when he says they were dividing incorrectly (poion oun de phrazeis diairoumenous hemas ouk orthos arti dran). This is an explicit request for correction of a methodological mistake."
textual_basis: At 262c, after the Stranger signals there is an error to explain, young Socrates asks which kind of incorrect division the Stranger is referring to. The question uses the first-person plural (hemas), treating the error as joint, and requests specification of the error type (poion).
limits: This records the formal move of soliciting correction; it does not evaluate whether young Socrates understands the error in advance or whether his question is genuine or feigned.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0037
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 262a-262b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 262a-262b
  start_marker: 262a
  end_marker: 262b
  start_char: 9429
  end_char: 10211
  text_sha256: ec3296fa13657236dc9827a2287cb1abdd26e92214e7a24dea4b6b4145092459
greek_terms:
  - μέρος
  - εἶδος
  - ἰδέαις
english_gloss: part, species, forms
observation: The Eleatic Visitor rejects the Young Socrates' division of living beings into humans and beasts on methodological grounds, arguing that a division should not separate a small part from large and many parts without the part being at the same time a species (eidos), and that dividing through the middle is safer for encountering forms (ideai).
textual_basis: "At 262a-262b the Visitor explicitly corrects the division just offered: one should not remove a small part from large and many without the part having eidos, and recommends cutting through the middle as safer for hitting upon ideai."
limits: This observation records the Visitor's methodological correction about diairetic procedure. It does not claim that the same criticism recurs in other dialogues, nor does it assess whether the Visitor's own subsequent divisions obey this rule.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0038
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 262a-262b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 262a-262b
  start_marker: 262a
  end_marker: 262b
  start_char: 9429
  end_char: 10211
  text_sha256: ec3296fa13657236dc9827a2287cb1abdd26e92214e7a24dea4b6b4145092459
greek_terms:
  - μέρος
  - εἶδος
  - ἰδέαις
english_gloss: part, species, forms
observation: The Eleatic Visitor draws an explicit methodological distinction between a mere part (meros) and a species (eidos), insisting that a division's part should at the same time be a species, and connects this to encountering forms (ideai) through safer diairetic procedure.
textual_basis: At 262b the Visitor states that the part should at the same time have eidos and that cutting through the middle makes one more likely to encounter ideai.
limits: This observation records the Visitor's explicit vocabulary connecting diairetic method to forms-language. It does not claim that this vocabulary maps onto the Theory of Forms from other dialogues, nor does it assert that the Visitor himself consistently uses these terms elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0039
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 262a-262b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  - προθυμότατα
  - ἀνδρειότατα
  - ἐπέσπευσας
english_gloss: most eagerly, most courageously, you hurried
observation: The Visitor praises the Young Socrates' division as most eager and most courageous, then immediately corrects it as hasty, noting that Socrates hurried the argument upon seeing it heading toward humans. The praise frames a methodological correction delivered through procedural criticism rather than refutation of a definition.
textual_basis: At 262a the Visitor calls the division most eager and most courageous but adds a caution. At 262b he says Socrates hurried the argument upon seeing it heading toward humans.
limits: This observation records the Visitor's combination of praise and procedural correction. It does not claim that this pattern of praise-then-correction is unique to the Statesman, nor does it analyze the Visitor's tone as irony.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0040
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 263b-263c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 263b-263c
  start_marker: 263b
  end_marker: 263c
  start_char: 11814
  end_char: 12683
  text_sha256: 2cbf3d5fea017b3967df660630494ca42a4a4370eab17ee04a031f5ad92e8347
greek_terms:
  - εἶδος
  - μέρος
english_gloss: form/species, part
observation: The Eleatic Stranger draws an explicit conceptual distinction between eidos (form/species) and meros (part) at 263b, asserting that every eidos is necessarily a part of the thing of which it is said to be an eidos, but a part is not necessarily an eidos.
textual_basis: "The Stranger states at 263b that eidos and meros are heteron allelon (different from each other), then provides an asymmetric necessity relation: when something is an eidos of X, it must be a part of X, but a part need not be an eidos. He insists the Younger Socrates always understand him as speaking in this way (tautei me e 'keinei mallon)."
limits: This observation records the conceptual distinction as stated; it does not assess whether the Stranger's asymmetry holds across the dialogue or whether the distinction is later revised.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The passage names the wandering away and asks for its point of origin before recalling the prior division into humans and all other animals.
limits: This record captures the discourse-management move only. It does not carry the substantive diagnosis of the division error.

```

```yaml
textual_basis: The passage recalls the two-part animal division, says the human part was removed, and explains that the rest appeared to be one kind only because a common animal name was applied to all of them.
limits: This does not restate the preceding abstract species/part rule, and it does not include the later counterexample about a nonhuman classifier.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0043
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 262e-263a

  start_char: 11020
  end_char: 11814
  text_sha256: 9057661a7c8bf0c51cc7dbc082f3c92981c783fa5ea10aed5748f293694cdb5a
greek_terms:
  - γένος
  - μέρος
  - εἶδος
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger raises the problem of distinguishing genus from part, noting that one may erroneously treat a part as a separate genus when one isolates it by name alone (262e). Young Socrates then asks how to recognize that genus and part are not the same but different from each other (263a).
textual_basis: At 262e the Stranger describes the error of separating something as a single form and giving the remainder a single name, claiming it to be a separate genus. At 263a Young Socrates asks how one might know more clearly that genus and part are not the same but different from each other.
limits: The passage introduces the genus-part distinction as a dialectical problem but does not resolve it within this segment. The observation does not claim that a definition is completed or that the Stranger provides a criterion for the distinction here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0044

  text_sha256: 9057661a7c8bf0c51cc7dbc082f3c92981c783fa5ea10aed5748f293694cdb5a
greek_terms:
  - ἀποχωρίζων
  - ἀποσχίζοι
  - σχισθέντων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger illustrates proper division with examples of cutting by even and odd for number, by male and female for the human genus, and contrasts these with improper division that separates one group (e.g. Lydians or Phrygians) against all the rest when one cannot find both a genus and a part in each of the split members (262e-263a).
textual_basis: At 262e the Stranger says that division would be finer and more according to forms if one cuts number by even and odd, the human genus by male and female, but that separating Lydians or Phrygians against all others is done when one is at a loss to find a genus and a part in each of the split items (262e-263a).
limits: The observation notes the normative claim about proper division method without assessing whether the examples themselves succeed as definitions. It does not claim that the Stranger resolves the genus-part problem in this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 262e-263a the Stranger says they have already wandered a longer path than needed from the proposed argument, and when Young Socrates asks for more, the Stranger says he is ordering them to wander still further, before saying 'let us go back' at 263a.
limits: This observation tracks the explicit meta-discursive turn-management language. It does not claim that the digression is resolved or that the return is successfully executed within this segment.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 264a-b, the Stranger says not to divide as before looking toward everything nor to hurry so that they reach the politike quickly. He then cites a proverb about those not dividing calmly finishing more slowly. Young Socrates approves the proverb's application.
limits: This observation records the explicit methodological caution at this point in the dialogue. It does not assess whether the dialogue as a whole follows this principle or whether the proverb is applied correctly.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  At 261d the Stranger states that one can see animal generation and nurture as either solitary or communal care of herd animals. At 261e he asks whether to call communal nurture of many together by one of two alternative names, making the division step explicit.
```

```yaml
  When young Socrates responds that either naming term is fine (261e), the Stranger praises him but counsels him not to be overly serious about names, adding that if he guards against this he will appear richer in wisdom in old age. The exchange constitutes a procedural instruction embedded within the division method.
textual_basis: |
  At 261e young Socrates says whichever term fits in the argument. The Stranger replies with approval and the counsel to avoid being serious about names, then resumes the division.
limits: |
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0053

stephanus_span: 265d-265e

  text_sha256: d882789fa9855d03eabf57563b7254abb6388f9c335b23a2a9c23629af95a7a0
greek_terms:
  - σχιστῷ
  - μώνυχι
  - κοινογονίᾳ
  - ἰδιογονίᾳ
english_gloss: split-hoofed vs. single-hoofed; common-birth vs. peculiar-birth
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger offers two alternative dichotomous division criteria to subdivide the hornless herd: by hoof-type (split-hoofed vs. single-hoofed / solid-hoofed) or by reproductive kind (common-genesis vs. peculiar-genesis). Only one criterion is pursued in the immediate sequel."
textual_basis: At 265d-e, the Stranger asks whether the young Socrates prefers to divide by to schistōi te kai tōi kaloumenōi mōnuchi (split-hoofed and so-called solid-hoofed) or by koinogoniai te kai idiogoniai (common-birth and peculiar-birth), then explains each pair. The explicit offering of two distinct diairetic axes is the textual phenomenon captured here.
limits: This observation records only the presentation of alternative division axes at 265d-265e. It does not assert which axis the dialogue ultimately follows, nor does it claim any methodological preference or hidden meaning behind the alternatives.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0054
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 265d-265e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 265d-265e
  start_marker: 265d
  end_marker: 265e
  start_char: 16729
  end_char: 17551
  text_sha256: d882789fa9855d03eabf57563b7254abb6388f9c335b23a2a9c23629af95a7a0
greek_terms:
  - κοινογονίᾳ
  - ἰδιογονίᾳ
  - ἀμιγὲς γένει
english_gloss: common-birth, peculiar-birth, unmixed in kind
observation: "The Stranger introduces interbreeding as a diairetic criterion: horses and donkeys can generate from one another (common-genesis), while the remainder of the smooth herd of tame animals is unmixed in kind toward one another (peculiar-genesis). The statesman's herd is identified with the unmixed kind."
textual_basis: At 265e, the Stranger explains that horses and donkeys by nature generate from each other (ex allēlōn gennan), whereas the rest of the smooth tame herd is amiges genei pros allēla (unmixed in kind toward one another). Young Socrates then affirms that the statesman's care concerns the unmixed (amiktou) nature.
limits: This observation records the interbreeding criterion as a specific diairetic device at 265d-265e. It does not evaluate whether this criterion is scientifically valid, nor does it compare this usage to other Platonic biological classifications.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - μεσοτομεῖν

  - μακροτέραν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 265a, the Eleatic Stranger presents an explicit methodological choice between two division procedures: a shorter, faster path that divides a small part from a large part, and a longer path that cuts through the middle, which he says they previously endorsed as the preferred method."
textual_basis: "The Stranger says two paths appear: the faster one dividing a small part from a large part, and the one that cuts through the middle, which is longer but more in line with what they said earlier about the need to cut through the middle as much as possible. Young Socrates asks whether both are possible, and the Stranger replies not at the same time but in succession (265a)."
limits: This observation records the explicit methodological contrast between two division procedures. It does not analyze which method is ultimately preferred or why.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The Stranger asks whether the political art is to be sought concerning the footed kind (264e), and states that foot-herding must be shown being cut in two, just as they did earlier with number (264e-265a). Young Socrates agrees.
limits: This records a single step in the ongoing dichotomous division. It does not characterize the full division tree or assess whether this step follows correctly from prior divisions.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 265b Young Socrates says he chooses both paths in turn (en merei amphoteras hairoumai). The Visitor agrees and decides to take the longer path first, noting it would have been difficult earlier but now they are fresher (nealesteroi) and will traverse it more easily.
```

```yaml
observation: The Stranger instructs Young Socrates to deliver the division to each part using an account rather than by naming, because naming would become more entangled than necessary.
textual_basis: At 265c the Stranger says to give each part through reasoning (logo chromenos), because if you try to name them it will be more entangled than needed (peripeplegmenon mallon tou deontos). He then models this by describing the parts rather than coining names.
limits: This is a methodological observation about the diairetic procedure, not a claim about the nature of naming as such.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 266a-266b the Stranger states that the two remaining kinds should be divided by the diagonal and the diagonal of the diagonal, then explicates this in terms of the human walk being analogous to the diagonal that is two-feet in power.
limits: This observation notes the presence of a geometric analogy for diairetic division; it does not assess whether the division is sound or whether the mathematical claim about the diagonal is correct.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0063
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 267b-267c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὸν
  - πολιτικόν
english_gloss: human-herding, kingly, and political
observation: |
  At 267b-267c, the Eleatic Stranger recapitulates the entire diairetic division that led to the definition of the statesman, compressing multiple branching steps into a single continuous sentence. The final cut isolates the human-herding art, which is identified as the long-sought object, simultaneously called kingly and political.
textual_basis: |
  The passage from 267b to 267c traces the division from animal-rearing through herd-rearing, land-herding, and the rearing of the hornless nature, finally arriving at the two-footed herd branch. The verbal chain uses a sequence of genitive-of-genitive constructions, compressing the earlier, more explicitly enumerated division from earlier in the dialogue into a dense summary. The concluding identification of the isolated branch with the kingly and political art is marked by the phrase at the start of 267c (tout' auto estin ede to zetēthen).
limits: |
  This observation records only the textual compression of the division chain at 267b-267c. It does not evaluate whether the division is logically adequate or whether the compression introduces ambiguity. The observation does not claim that the compression is a rhetorical or esoteric device.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Rejected repair: this record converts local hornless and unmixed wording into a forms-trajectory vocabulary note and overstates a terminological pattern."
textual_basis: The span supports a compressed recap of the animal-rearing division and a label for the unmixed-generation herding branch. That division-chain compression is already retained in obs_statesman_0063, and the forms-trajectory framing is not locally warranted.
limits: No accepted replacement is added because the durable classification content is already covered and the rejected record's theoretical family is too strong for the span.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0069
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 263d-263e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 263d-263e
  start_marker: 263d
  end_marker: 263e
  start_char: 12683
  end_char: 13434
  text_sha256: 78b80b48e79bd59c35893355f76bbf397c3b75a8ec0c7cb47595f9a1ce961180
greek_terms:
  - γένος
  - θηρία
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger uses a counterfactual about cranes to expose the risk of self-favoring bias in dichotomous division. He imagines a prudent animal like the crane making its own kind one genus and lumping everything else, including humans, under 'beasts'.
textual_basis: "At 263d the Stranger hypothesizes that if another animal were intelligent, it would counterpose its own kind as a single genus against all others and label the rest, including humans, as beasts. He then draws a methodological lesson at 263e: we should be cautious not to divide the whole genus of animals in a self-serving way, since the error occurred earlier in their own division."
limits: This passage uses a humorous counterfactual to illustrate a division error; it does not assert that cranes actually reason this way or that humans are equivalent to beasts in any biological sense. The observation is limited to the methodological critique of dichotomous division exposed to anthropocentric bias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0070
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 263d-263e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 263d-263e
  start_marker: 263d
  end_marker: 263e
  start_char: 12683
  end_char: 13434
  text_sha256: 78b80b48e79bd59c35893355f76bbf397c3b75a8ec0c7cb47595f9a1ce961180
greek_terms:
  - ὦ πάντων ἀνδρειότατε
  - πειραθῶμεν οὖν ἡμεῖς ἐξευλαβεῖσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger addresses the young Socrates with an exaggerated honorific, calling him 'bravest of all men,' at the very moment he is about to correct the prior division error that the young Socrates had himself made by calling all non-human animals 'beasts.'
textual_basis: At 263d the Stranger says 'bravest of all men' right before showing that the young Socrates' own division could be replicated by any intelligent animal against humans. The superlativity is ironic because the division being critiqued is the one the young Socrates produced, and the correction shows why it was not brave or wise but self-serving.
limits: The phrase could also function as a genuine friendly tease rather than biting irony. The observation records the mismatch between the superlative address and the impending correction, not a claim about the Stranger's psychological state.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἐξευλαβεῖσθαι
  - διαιρούμενοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger interrupts the division to issue a methodological caution: he calls for vigilance against self-serving partition when dividing kinds, and proposes a remedy — not dividing the whole genus — before returning to the earlier division for correction."
textual_basis: At 263d-e the Stranger says 'let us try to beware of all such things' and when asked how, specifies 'by not dividing the whole genus of animals, so that we are less affected by this.' He then connects this back to the earlier error and resumes the division at 263e.
limits: This is a local procedural intervention embedded in a longer diairetic process; it does not establish a permanent method for all divisions. The specific remedy is tied to the animal-herding division, not a blanket rule for all diairesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger groups multiple traditional mythic phenomena (earthborn men born from the earth rather than from one another) as stemming from a single cause (tauton pathos) that no one has yet explained.
textual_basis: At 269b the Stranger lists the race of earthborn men (gēgeneis) as one of the ancient tales, then asserts that all these things (sumpanta) arise from the same condition (ek tautou pathous), adding that the cause (to pathos) of all these things has never been stated (269c).
limits: The observation does not characterize the upcoming myth or evaluate whether the Stranger's account is itself mythic. It notes only the framing device of bundling received myths under a single unexplained cause.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0075
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 268a-268b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 268a-268b
  start_marker: 268a

  start_char: 21813

  text_sha256: e91e72509793d15b7aa33a62baecf93f5487b374d002e6fb1cf74614ed1b8f67
greek_terms:
  - βουφορβός
  - ἰατρός
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger presents the cowherd as an analogical paradigm in which a single practitioner performs multiple sub-functions for the herd without any dispute from rival claimants.
textual_basis: |
  At 268a-268b the Stranger enumerates that the cowherd (herdsman) alone is nurse, doctor, matchmaker, midwifery expert, and musician for soothing the herd, and that no one disputes the cowherd's claim to any of these roles. The enumeration of sub-crafts within one paradigmatic craft domain is the basis for the analogy to the statesman.
limits: |
  The passage establishes the cowherd as a paradigm but does not yet map each enumerated sub-function onto the statesman or argue for the analogy's validity. The observation records the structural presentation of the paradigm, not its subsequent application.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0076
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 268a-268b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 268a-268b
  start_marker: 268a

  end_char: 22634
  text_sha256: e91e72509793d15b7aa33a62baecf93f5487b374d002e6fb1cf74614ed1b8f67
greek_terms:
  - ἀμφισβητήσει
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The passage contrasts the undisputed comprehensive care of the cowherd with the disputed, multi-claimant care of humans, using the uncontested paradigm to frame the definitional problem for the statesman.
textual_basis: |
  At 268a the Stranger notes that many claimants would dispute each other over care of humans, not only herd-like humans but their rulers too. At 268a-268b he contrasts this with the cowherd, for whom no one disputes any of the enumerated care functions. The contrast sets up the cowherd as an uncontested model against which the contested definition of the statesman can be pursued.
limits: |
  The observation records the structural contrast that frames the definitional search. It does not claim that the cowherd paradigm succeeds in resolving the dispute, nor does it assess whether the contrast is persuasive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  When the Stranger grants that the many claimants to human care might be speaking correctly, he immediately qualifies with a hedging term and announces that this point will be examined later, deferring a challenge to the current line of argument.
textual_basis: |
  At 268a the Stranger responds to the suggestion that the claimants speak correctly with a term meaning perhaps and states that this will be examined. The deferral preserves the current argumentative trajectory while flagging a point that requires later scrutiny.
limits: |
  The observation records the procedural deferral. It does not track whether the deferred examination is later carried out, nor does it assess whether the deferral is merely rhetorical.

```

```yaml
greek_terms:

  - παιδιὰς ἔτη
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 268e, the Eleatic Stranger announces that he is about to tell a myth and instructs the Younger Socrates to pay close attention like children do, adding that Socrates has not yet escaped the years of childish play.
textual_basis: The Stranger says 'pay very close attention to my myth, like children; for you have not entirely escaped the years of childish play' (268e). This frames myth-reception as requiring a childlike attentiveness and explicitly links the interlocutor's relative youth to his fitness for receiving myth.
limits: This observation does not claim that the myth's content is childish or that Plato devalues myth. It notes only the framing instruction given to the interlocutor.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0079

  end_char: 24320
  text_sha256: 9fb8c187ceebfe585d5bd022ad43d06e7335e0ac48c242b9829013be5a5c5cee
greek_terms:
  - φάσμα
  - οὐδαμῶς
  - χρυσῆς ἀρνὸς
  - μεταβολῆς δύσεώς τε καὶ ἀνατολῆς ἡλίου
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 268e-269a, the Stranger references the phantom concerning the strife of Atreus and Thyestes. The Younger Socrates guesses he means the sign of the golden lamb, but the Stranger emphatically denies this and specifies he means the story of the reversal of the sun's rising and setting.
textual_basis: The Stranger asks 'you have surely heard and remember what they say happened then' (268e). The Younger Socrates guesses 'the sign concerning the golden lamb perhaps' (268e). The Stranger replies 'not at all, but the story about the change of the setting and rising of the sun' (269a). This constitutes an explicit correction of a misidentified mythic reference.
limits: This observation does not explain why the Stranger prefers one myth over the other. It notes only the dialogic pattern of misidentification and correction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - Ἀτρέως
  - Θυέστου
  - μεταβολῆς
  - βασιλείαν
  - Κρόνος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "In the span 268e-269a, the Stranger enumerates three distinct mythic references in quick succession: the phantom concerning the strife of Atreus and Thyestes, the reversal of the sun's course at the time of Atreus, and the kingship ruled by Kronos."
textual_basis: The Stranger references 'the phantom concerning the strife of Atreus and Thyestes' (268e), 'the change of the setting and rising of the sun and the other stars' (269a), and 'the kingship which Kronos ruled' (269a). Each is introduced as something the interlocutor has heard from tradition.
limits: This observation does not claim that these myths are thematically connected or that they function identically in the argument. It notes only the rapid enumeration of multiple mythic references.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - θειοτάτοις

observation: The Stranger asserts that remaining always in the same state and in the same way and being the same belongs only to the most divine things, while bodily nature is explicitly excluded from this order (269d-e).
textual_basis: At 269d-e the Stranger states that remaining in the same respects and in the same way always and being the same befits only the most divine things, but the nature of body is not of this order.
limits: This is a claim within the cosmological myth; it does not necessarily represent a final metaphysical doctrine. The observation records the stated principle, not a systematic theory of forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0082
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  - κεκοινώνηκέ
  - σώματος
  - γεννήσαντος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Within the cosmological myth, the Stranger explains that the cosmos reverses its rotation because, although it has received many blessed attributes from its begetter, it also participates in body and therefore cannot be entirely without change (269d-e).
textual_basis: At 269d-e the Stranger says that the heaven and cosmos, though it has received many blessed things from its begetter, nevertheless participates also in body, and from this it is impossible for it to be without a share of change entirely.
limits: This is an explanation offered within the mythic narrative of cosmic cycles, not a deductive proof. The observation records the mythic explanation as given, not its truth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: The Stranger describes the cosmic reversal as causing mass extinction of animals and near-extinction of the human race.
textual_basis: At 270c-d the Stranger states that during the cosmic reversal "the greatest destructions of necessity occur to the other animals, and indeed the human race is left only in small number" (270c-d). The passage explicitly links cosmic-level change to biological catastrophe.
limits: This observation identifies the catastrophic destruction motif within the cosmological myth. It does not claim any particular interpretive significance for the motif.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger describes how, during the cosmic reversal, all living things cease aging and then reverse their biological development toward youth.
textual_basis: At 270d the Stranger states that during the reversal "whatever age each living thing had, this first of all stood still for everything, and every mortal thing ceased going toward appearing older, and changing back again toward the opposite direction" (270d). The cessation of aging and reversal of the life-cycle are presented as consequences of the cosmic turning.
limits: This observation records the age-standstill and reversal motif as a checkable textual feature within the cosmological myth. It does not interpret the motif's philosophical or esoteric significance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This observation records the turn-geometry pattern of minimal respondent assent during a sustained narrative passage. It does not claim that the respondent's assent is insincere or that the pattern carries hidden dramatic meaning.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 270a-270b, the Stranger uses a tote men...tote de alternation to describe the cosmos as sometimes guided by a divine cause and sometimes released to go by itself. Young Socrates at 270b confirms the account as plausible (eikotos).

review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0087

stephanus_span: 270a-270b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 270a-270b
  start_marker: 270a
  end_marker: 270b
  start_char: 26020
  end_char: 26862
  text_sha256: ac4349022964fd2d06fbba78ab2e62d11b1a652c2788b6dabe6243626b5ea0a0
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργοῦ
  - ἀθανασίαν ἐπισκευαστὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The cosmos receives repaired immortality from the demiurge, framing the divine cause as a craftsman who repairs or refits the cosmos.
textual_basis: At 270a, the Stranger says the cosmos receives life again and acquires repaired immortality from the demiurge (para tou demiourgou). The term demiourgos is the standard Greek word for a craftsman or artisan, and episkeuasten means repaired or refitted, reinforcing the craft metaphor.
limits: This observation registers the craft language at 270a but does not analyze how the Statesman's demiurge compares to the Timaeus demiurge or whether the craft analogy holds throughout the myth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger rules out the possibility that two gods with opposed intentions turn the cosmos, settling on the remaining account: alternation between divine guidance and self-motion."
textual_basis: At 270a, the Stranger explicitly negates the alternative of two gods turning the cosmos in opposite directions and pivots with "but just what was said before" to retain the single remaining account. This is a process of elimination rather than a demonstration.
limits: This observation notes the logical structure of eliminating alternatives but does not assess whether the retained account is sound or whether the elimination exhausts the possibilities.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0089

greek_terms:
  - νεογενοῦς
  - παιδὸς
  - φύσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger describes a detailed physiological reversal during the age of Kronos: white hair turns black, bearded cheeks become smooth, youthful bodies shrink toward newborn form, and victims of violent death rapidly disappear."
textual_basis: At 270e-271a, the narrative catalogs hair color reversal, cheek smoothing, body diminution toward infant form, and the rapid vanishing of violently killed bodies.
limits: This observation records the presence of a mythic reversal narrative; it does not assert Plato's cosmological commitments.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: When the younger Socrates asks how living beings were generated in that era, the Stranger denies mutual reproduction and instead identifies the earth-born race arising again from the earth.
textual_basis: At 271a, the Stranger states there was no generation from one another in that nature and substitutes the earth-born race rising from earth.
limits: This records a mythic claim about autochthony; it does not assess its truth or its role in the dialogue's political argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 271a, the Stranger says the earth-born race was remembered by our earliest ancestors.
limits: This observation records the citation of ancestral authority; it does not assess the reliability of the tradition or whether the Stranger endorses it.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0092
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 268c-268d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 268c-268d
  start_marker: 268c
  end_marker: 268d
  start_char: 22634
  end_char: 23459
  text_sha256: 50dcc7076b7d56b83da5521872f9ecf18f7bc8bf76e2c3d7caaa745e2a622119
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger reports that they earlier feared their account had only produced a mere outline (schema basilikon) of the statesman, not a finished precise account; he now declares the necessity of stripping away competing claimants and separating the statesman from them before the account can be made pure.
textual_basis: At 268c the Stranger asks whether they should worry that, by setting down the king as herdsman and nourisher of the human herd (nomea kai trophon ageles anthropines), they have merely singled him out from countless other claimants without completing the account. He recalls having feared earlier that they were saying something of the royal shape but had not yet worked out the politikos with precision. At 268d he insists that the task must be done so as not to disgrace the argument at its end (me mellomen epi toi telei kataischynai ton logon).
limits: This record identifies a procedural aporia or impasse concerning the sufficiency of the definitional work done so far, not a substantive aporia about the nature of the statesman itself. The Stranger does not claim the inquiry has failed, only that a new path is required.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0093
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 268c-268d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 268c-268d
  start_marker: 268c
  end_marker: 268d
  start_char: 22634
  end_char: 23459
  text_sha256: 50dcc7076b7d56b83da5521872f9ecf18f7bc8bf76e2c3d7caaa745e2a622119
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger declares that they must proceed again from another starting-point down a different road (palin toinyn ex alles arches dei kath' heteran hodon poreuthenai tina) and signals a major methodological shift: they will mix in play (paidia) and use a large myth."
textual_basis: "At 268d, immediately after the young Socrates agrees that the purification of the account must be done, the Stranger says: 'palin toinyn ex alles arches dei kath' heteran hodon poreuthenai tina.' When asked what kind of road, he answers: 'schedon paidian enkerasamenous; sychnoi gar merei dei megalou mythou proschresasthai' — they must mix in something close to play and make use of a great myth for the remaining part."
limits: This records an explicit restart of the methodological path, not a conclusion. The observation identifies the formal announcement of a new procedure; it does not evaluate whether the mythic method is adequate or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0094

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger announces that the remaining argument will mix in play (paidia) and require the use of a large myth (megalou mythou), drawing an explicit boundary between the preceding argumentative work and a newly introduced mythic register.
textual_basis: "At 268d the Stranger says, in response to the young Socrates asking what kind of road they must now travel: 'schedon paidian enkerasamenous; sychnoi gar merei dei megalou mythou proschresasthai' — they must mix in play and make use of a great myth for much of what remains. The transition is self-consciously marked as a shift in discursive mode."
limits: This records the explicit boundary drawn between a prior dialectical or definitional procedure and the myth about to be introduced. It does not assess the content of that myth, nor does it claim the myth replaces argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0095
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 271d-271e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 271d-271e
  start_marker: 271d
  end_marker: 271e
  start_char: 29556
  end_char: 30410
  text_sha256: d51f45145f6e008e846771f4aafa19ee8dd460d352effbde0b83f7563c20a9dc
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger describes a prior cosmic age in which a god (theos) governed the whole revolution of the cosmos, with subordinate gods and divine daimones presiding over the parts of the cosmos and tending each kind of living creature as herdsmen.
textual_basis: At 271d the Stranger states that in the former age the god himself first ruled and supervised the whole revolution (kyklesis), and that by local arrangement all parts of the cosmos were divided under ruling gods, with divine daimones distributing the animals by kind and herd like herdsmen. At 271e he adds that in these conditions there was nothing wild, no eating of one another, and no war or faction at all.
limits: This observation records only the structural outline of the myth as delivered by the Stranger; it does not assess whether the myth is offered as literal history, allegory, or a playful illustration for the political argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0096
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 271d-271e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 271d-271e
  start_marker: 271d

  start_char: 29556
  end_char: 30410
  text_sha256: d51f45145f6e008e846771f4aafa19ee8dd460d352effbde0b83f7563c20a9dc
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger frames the myth of the spontaneous life by explicitly contrasting the former cosmic age (the age of Kronos) with the present established course (tes nyn kathestekyias phoras), marking the current age as different in kind from the mythic one.
textual_basis: At 271d the Stranger says that the account of all things coming to be spontaneously for human beings belongs 'least of all to the present established course (hekista tes nyn esti kathestekyias phoras) but was also part of the former one.' This temporal contrast between the mythic past and the present age structures the whole narration.
limits: This observation notes only the explicit temporal demarcation between cosmic ages. It does not evaluate whether the contrast implies a historical claim, an allegorical device, or a logical concession for the sake of the definition search.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0097
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 271d-271e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 271d-271e
  start_marker: 271d
  end_marker: 271e
  start_char: 29556
  end_char: 30410
  text_sha256: d51f45145f6e008e846771f4aafa19ee8dd460d352effbde0b83f7563c20a9dc
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger casts divine rule in the prior age through a herdsmanship analogy: the god tends human beings as a herdsman (nomeus), and the divine daimones tend the other kinds of animals by genus and herd (kata gene kai agelas), just as human beings now tend other, lesser kinds of animals."
textual_basis: At 271d the divine daimones are said to have distributed the animals kata gene kai agelas like divine herdsmen (hoion nomes theioi). At 271e the Stranger states that the god tended human beings himself, just as now human beings — a more divine animal — tend other, baser genera (alla gene phaulotera hauton nomeuousi).
limits: This observation records the explicit analogy drawn between divine care and herdsmanship. It does not assess how the analogy constrains or undermines the search for the statesman as a human herdsman, nor whether the analogy is intended to collapse later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 272d, the speaker says they must state why the myth was roused, marking a transition from myth narration to myth application.
limits: This does not establish whether the myth is presented as true or false, only that its narrative purpose is made explicit.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 272d, the speaker says to leave these things aside until a sufficient informant appears regarding how the people of that age stood toward knowledge and discourse.
limits: This observation does not address whether the deferral is ironic or sincere, only that epistemological limits are placed on myth content within the myth narrative itself.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0100

greek_terms:
  - κυβερνήτης
  - πηδαλίων οἴακος
  - περιωπὴν
  - εἱμαρμένη
  - σύμφυτος ἐπιθυμία
english_gloss: pilot, rudder handles, lookout, fate, innate desire
observation: The Stranger describes the divine pilot of the universe releasing the rudder handles and withdrawing to his lookout at 272e, after which the cosmos is turned back by fate and an innate desire. The nautical craft of steering is used as an image for the cosmic governance that the god relinquishes.
textual_basis: At 272e the Stranger narrates the moment when the divine pilot lets go of the rudder and the cosmos reverses direction under the impulse of fate and innate desire rather than divine guidance. The vocabulary draws on nautical language to frame the transition from divine governance to immanent cosmic forces.
limits: This observation notes the presence of nautical craft imagery in the cosmic narrative. It does not assess whether the myth as a whole is an allegory, nor does it interpret the theological implications of the pilot's withdrawal.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0101
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 272e-273a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
```
