```yaml
  text_sha256: 11e26ecf836249538564b02ad9a57387b70f0593c3ace2c3f1e8610cb7a02d8c
greek_terms:
  - σεισμὸν πολὺν
  - φθορὰν ζῴων παντοίων
  - γαλήνης
  - κατακοσμούμενος
english_gloss: great earthquake, destruction of all kinds of living things, calm, ordering itself
observation: At 273a the cosmos undergoes a violent reversal described as producing a great earthquake and another destruction of all kinds of living things. After sufficient time, the cosmos achieves calm and proceeds in its customary course, ordering itself. This sequence forms part of the Stranger's mythic narrative of the alternating cosmic cycle.
textual_basis: The passage 273a describes the violent consequences of the cosmic reversal — earthquake and destruction of living things — followed by a return to order and calm. This continues the mythic narrative introduced earlier in the Statesman about the two alternating cosmic periods, with the present passage marking the transition point between divine governance and the reverse cycle.
limits: This observation records the narrative content of the cosmic reversal myth. It does not determine whether the myth is meant literally or allegorically, nor does it assess the philosophical validity of the cycle model.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0102
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 273b-273c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 273b-273c
  start_marker: 273b
  end_marker: 273c
  start_char: 32909
  end_char: 33702
  text_sha256: a7e4818d3baf7eb2089a294f40a4b3b4e6e4e5206de0ef1750fd4670c47173c3
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργοῦ
  - πατρὸς
  - ἀπομνημονεύων διδαχὴν
  - ἀκριβέστερον
  - ἀμβλύτερον
  - λήθης
  - ἀναρμοστίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The cosmos is depicted as an apprentice that recalls and enacts the teaching of the demiurge and father (273b). It performs more precisely at first, then more dully as time passes and forgetfulness grows in it, causing the ancient disharmony to dominate (273c).
textual_basis: |
  At 273b, the cosmos is said to recall the teaching of the demiurge and father and to perform it according to its capacity. The text explicitly marks a precision gradient: initially more exact, eventually blunter. At 273c, the mechanism is identified as progressive forgetfulness that enables the ancient disharmony to gain power.
limits: |
  The observation does not claim that this apprenticeship model is the only craft analogy in the myth. It does not address whether the cosmos is itself a living being or a mechanism.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0103
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 273b-273c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 273b-273c
  start_marker: 273b
  end_marker: 273c
  start_char: 32909
  end_char: 33702
  text_sha256: a7e4818d3baf7eb2089a294f40a4b3b4e6e4e5206de0ef1750fd4670c47173c3
greek_terms:
  - συνθέντος
  - πάντα καλὰ
  - χαλεπὰ καὶ ἄδικα
  - ἀταξίας
  - σωματοειδὲς
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The myth explains the mixed condition of the cosmos by tracing goods and evils to two distinct sources: all beautiful things come from the composer (the demiurge), while harsh and unjust things in heaven derive from the prior condition of disorder that the cosmos inherited from its bodily admixture (273b-273c).
textual_basis: |
  At 273b, the cause of the cosmos's declining precision is identified as the bodily element of its mixture, which was raised together with the ancient nature and participated in much disorder before arriving at the present cosmos. At 273b-273c, a strict dichotomy is drawn: from the composer come all beautiful things, while from the prior condition come whatever harsh and unjust things arise in heaven, which the cosmos both has itself and produces in living beings.
limits: |
  The observation records the dual-source structure of the mythic explanation without evaluating whether this constitutes a theodicy or whether the demiurge is thereby absolved of responsibility for evil. It does not address how this dualism relates to other Platonic accounts of evil.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This observation notes only the explicit purpose marker; it does not assess whether the myth actually fulfills its stated purpose.
```

```yaml

  - ἀφύλακτοι

observation: "The Stranger explains early human vulnerability after the caretaker daemon withdrew: wild animals became dangerous, humans were weak and unprotected, and they lacked the means and crafts needed for self-support."
textual_basis: The passage links the loss of divine care with hostile animals, defenseless humans, lack of automatic food, lack of provisioning skill, and severe difficulty.
limits: This records a mythic cause for the human condition. It does not evaluate the truth of the myth or include the following catalogue of divine gifts.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 274c-d the text calls these 'ancient gifts from the gods' (para theon dora) given with 'necessary teaching and education' (met' anankaias didaches kai paideuseos), enumerating specific donors each with a distinct gift type.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0107
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 274c-274d

  - ἐπιμέλεια
  - συμμιμούμενοι καὶ συνεπόμενοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The passage describes the post-reversal human condition: early humans were without resource and without craft after spontaneous food ceased, divine care departed from humanity, and humans then had to manage themselves by imitating and following the cosmos."
textual_basis: At 274c-d the text states that divine care 'left humans' (epelipen anthropous) and they had to 'hold their own way of life and care for themselves' (di' heauton te edei ten te diagogen kai ten epimeleian autous hauton echein), 'imitating and following' (symmimoumenoi kai synepomenoi) the whole cosmos.
limits: This observation does not address the physical mechanism of cosmic reversal or evaluate the myth's truth claims.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - διημαρτάνομεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'The Eleatic Stranger announces that he and the Young Socrates made an error in a prior step of the diairetic division: they applied the name "herd-nurturing" to all herdsmen, but this name did not properly belong to the statesman. The Stranger identifies the specific mistake and begins to correct it by proposing broader alternative names at 275e.'
textual_basis: At 275d the Stranger uses the verb for having erred and explains that the statesman was never captured or named by their procedure, escaping through the naming. At 275e he proposes alternatives such as "herd-care," "tending," or "superintendence" that would cover what is common to all herdsmen.
limits: This observation records an explicit diairetic error announcement; it does not assess whether the corrected naming resolves the problem or whether the error is ultimately irreparable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger explicitly thematizes the role of naming within the diairetic procedure, stating that the statesman escaped them through the naming. He then discusses at 275e how a broader name (such as "herd-care," "tending," or "superintendence") would have properly covered all herdsmen including the statesman.
textual_basis: At 275d the Stranger says the statesman escaped them through the naming (275d). At 275e he lists candidate broader names and notes that a common name covering all should have been applied.
limits: This observation records that naming is treated as a methodological concern in the diairetic procedure; it does not claim that the dialogue develops a systematic theory of naming.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0110
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 275b-275c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  start_marker: 275b
  end_marker: 275c
  start_char: 37212
  end_char: 38058
  text_sha256: 19c485f6e746937c3f99ac862f79ecc9a4d20749c8b476937d36390eb5a6a97e
greek_terms:
  - θείου νομέως
  - πολιτικοὺς
english_gloss: divine herdsman, politicians
observation: |
  At 275b-c the Eleatic Stranger explicitly distinguishes the scale of the divine herdsman figure from that of the ordinary human politikos, claiming that the paradigm of the divine herdsman is far greater than any basileus ("this pattern of the divine herdsman is still greater than that of a king," 275b-c), and asserting that present-day politicoi are actually far more similar in nature and upbringing to their subjects.
textual_basis: |
  The Stranger states directly that the schema of the divine herdsman is "greater than that of a king" (275b-c) and then asserts that current human politicoi resemble their subjects in nature and have similarly shared education and nurture. This contrast between the superhuman paradigm and the actual human political class is made explicit in the dialogue's procedural movement.
limits: |
  The observation does not address the myth's content but only the explicit comparison of scale between the mythic herdsman and the human ruler. The passage is only one statement in the dialogue and does not itself resolve the relation between divine and human rule.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The Stranger states 'let the myth have its end' and immediately follows with 'we shall make it useful for discerning how much we went wrong,' establishing an explicit boundary where myth concludes and dialectical correction begins at 274e.
limits: This observation notes the structural boundary marker; it does not evaluate the content of the myth or the correctness of the self-diagnosis that follows.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0112

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 274e-275a the Stranger diagnoses an error in the earlier definition of the politician and proposes a corrective restart by distinguishing the manner of rule over the city.
textual_basis: The Stranger identifies that in the earlier account they said the ruler of the whole city was the shepherd of the human herd from the opposite cosmic period, confusing a god for a mortal (274e-275a). He then proposes that by determining the manner of rule they can complete the account at 275a. The younger Socrates assents.
limits: Records the procedural restart; does not assess whether the self-diagnosed error exhausts the problems with the earlier account or whether the proposed corrective method succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0113
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 274e-275a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 274e-275a the Stranger distinguishes two respects in which the earlier account erred: a major error of kind (confusing the divine shepherd of the reverse cosmic period with the mortal ruler of the present period) and a minor error of incompleteness (stating that the ruler governs the whole city without specifying the manner of rule)."
textual_basis: "The Stranger says the error was 'in one way smaller, in another way very noble and much larger' at 274e. He explains: speaking of the shepherd of the human herd from the opposite period, 'and that too a god instead of a mortal,' was a vast error; but stating the ruler governs the whole city without specifying the manner was true but not whole or clear, hence a smaller error at 275a."
limits: Notes the structural distinction between two kinds of error; does not evaluate whether this distinction adequately captures all deficiencies of the earlier account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - διημαρτάνετο
  - ἀποτετελεσμένην
  - βασιλικὴν
  - πολιτικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Visitor identifies an error in the preceding diairetic procedure at 276c: the art of the biped herd was prematurely named 'kingly' and 'political' (basiliken... kai politiken) as if the division were complete, when further cuts were still needed."
textual_basis: The Visitor explicitly marks the error at 276c with 'pros autoi de toi telei sychnon au diemartaneo' (at the very end, it was again much mistaken) and explains at 276c-d that they should not have called it kingly and political 'hos apotetelesmenen' (as if completed).
limits: The observation records an in-text self-correction within the Visitor's procedure, not a judgment about the correctness of the division method itself.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - μετασκευωρήσασθαι
  - ἐπιμέλειαν
  - τροφήν
  - θρεπτικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 276c-d, the Visitor proposes a methodological name change from threptike (nourishing) to epimeletike (caring), shifting the term from 'nourishment' (trophe) toward 'care' (epimeleia) to better capture the art's function before proceeding with further diairetic cuts.
textual_basis: "The Visitor states at 276c-d: 'tounoma metasketoresasthai, pros ten epimeleian mallon prosagagontas e ten trophen' (to refashion the name, bringing it closer to care rather than nourishment). This name change is presented as a preliminary methodological step ('proton men') before further cutting ('epeita tauten temnein')."
limits: This observation notes the renaming as a procedural move internal to the diairetic method; it does not assess whether the new name is adequate or final.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0116
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 276c-276d

greek_terms:
  - βιαίῳ
  - ἑκουσίῳ
  - τέμνειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 276d, after separating the divine herdsman from the human caretaker, the Visitor proposes dividing the human caretaking art (epimeletike) dichotomously by the criterion of violent (biaion) versus voluntary (hekousion).
textual_basis: "The Visitor states at 276d: 'toi biaioi te kai hekousioi' (by the violent and the voluntary) as the cutting criterion for the assigned caretaking art, following the pattern of dichotomous division that structures the dialogue's method. This is presented as a necessary next cut: 'temnein anankaion en' (it was necessary to cut)."
limits: The observation records the proposal of a dichotomous cut; it does not track whether this cut is actually executed or whether it survives later revision.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: The Visitor characterizes his own cosmological narrative as a myth and criticizes it as overextended and unfinished.
textual_basis: At 277b the Visitor uses the phrase "taking up the wondrous bulk of the myth" and states "we were forced to use a larger part of it than necessary." At the 277b-277c boundary he says "we did not put an end to the myth."
limits: This does not mean the myth is rejected as false; the Visitor critiques its proportion, not its truth-value.

```

```yaml
observation: The Visitor compares the incomplete logos to a living creature whose outline is adequate but which lacks the vividness that comes from pigments and color-blending, using a painting or sculpture craft analogy.
textual_basis: At 277c the Visitor says the logos is like a living creature, having sufficient external outline but not yet receiving vividness like that from pigments and blending of colors.
limits: This is an analogy about the incompleteness of the present logos, not a general theory of language or art.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Young Socrates acknowledges the Visitor's self-critique as correct but immediately requests that the Visitor specify what has not yet been adequately said, steering the conversation toward further elucidation.
textual_basis: At 277c Young Socrates responds "That is correct; but show in what respect you say it has not yet been adequately said to us."
limits: This does not indicate whether Young Socrates agrees with the Visitor's diagnosis of the myth's incompleteness; he only agrees the self-assessment was correct and asks for specification.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0120
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 276e-277a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 276e-277a

  text_sha256: c73c319abdbaf6179bd149f441320de1cfe3cbfcb83a5221034a1d29c126cbb4
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιμελητικὴν
  - βιαίῳ
  - ἑκουσίῳ
  - τυραννικήν
  - πολιτικήν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 276e-277a the Eleatic Stranger corrects a prior error and performs a fresh dichotomous division of human care-taking (epimeletike) into the forcible (biaion) and the voluntary (hekousion), assigning tyrannical rule to the forcible branch and political rule to the voluntary branch of herd-tending of two-footed animals.
textual_basis: The Stranger explicitly states they earlier erred by combining king and tyrant (276e). He then proposes dividing epimeletike by the forcible/voluntary criterion, names the two resulting kinds (tyrannike and politike), and declares the holder of the voluntary branch as true king and statesman.
limits: This observation records the dichotomous division structure and its correction of a prior error; it does not assess whether the division itself is adequate or final within the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0121

  - σχῆμα
  - πλείω
  - μείζω
  - μύθου
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger compares their overextended demonstration to sculptors who add too many and too large parts at the wrong moment, saying the king's figure is not yet complete.
textual_basis: The analogy names sculptors adding more than needed, then applies the same fault to the use of an oversized myth in the current demonstration.
limits: This records the craft analogy for excessive elaboration. It does not assess whether the myth is philosophically necessary or include the prior division of human care.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0122
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 276e-277a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  text_sha256: c73c319abdbaf6179bd149f441320de1cfe3cbfcb83a5221034a1d29c126cbb4
greek_terms:
  - κοινῇ συνδοκεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 277a, after the younger Socrates agrees that the demonstration about the statesman appears complete, the Eleatic Stranger insists that the agreement must not be his alone but must be held jointly (koine syndokein) by both interlocutors, and adds that in his own view the king does not yet have a complete shape.
textual_basis: The Stranger says dei de me soi mono tauta, alla kamoi meta sou koine syndokein, explicitly requiring joint assent and not merely the respondent's concurrence, before introducing the sculptor analogy to justify his reservation.
limits: This observation records the procedural demand for joint agreement on a conclusion. It does not claim that the demand is satisfied or that the definition is ultimately rejected.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: paradigm/example
observation: The Eleatic Visitor presents the use of paradeigmata as a method for adequately exhibiting something about larger matters and then introduces a specific example involving children learning letters.
textual_basis: At 277d the Visitor explicitly states the difficulty of demonstrating anything about larger matters without using paradeigmata. At 277e he begins a concrete example about children learning stoicheia (letters) in short, easy syllables, which is presented as a paradeigma for the larger epistemological point.
limits: This observation records the use of a paradigm method and a specific illustrative example. It does not claim that the Visitor's reference to the dream/waking contrast is itself the central argument of the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0124

  - ὄναρ
  - ὕπαρ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger compares the human difficulty in showing greater matters without paradigms to knowing everything as in a dream and then being ignorant again as if awake.
textual_basis: At 277d, the Stranger says that without paradigms each person seems to know everything dreamwise and then again to be ignorant of it as though awake, introducing the need for a paradigm of the paradigm itself.
limits: This records the dream-waking simile for an epistemic condition. It does not treat the passage as a myth narrative or as a developed doctrine of appearance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0125
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 278c-278d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 278c-278d
  start_marker: 278c
  end_marker: 278d
  start_char: 43569
  end_char: 44318
  text_sha256: b989101bc53922aa524c174e869d18642d7b08146d5a3660ead2a148b8de82cf
greek_terms:
  - γένεσις
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger provides an explicit definition of when a paradigm comes into being: when something that is the same, correctly opined in another scattered thing and brought together concerning each, produces one true opinion about both as a pair."
textual_basis: "At 278c the Stranger says they have sufficiently grasped this point (this is marked by hikanos suneilephamen) and then formulates the condition: a paradigm's genesis occurs whenever being the same in another scattered thing, correctly opined and brought together concerning each, as both together it produces one true opinion (mian alethe doxan). Young Socrates assents with phainetai."
limits: This is a formal definition of paradigm genesis, not an application of a specific paradigm to a target. The definition describes the cognitive mechanism of paradigm recognition but does not specify what the paradigm is a paradigm of.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0126
source_work: Statesman

  - στοιχεῖα
  - συλλαβὰς
  - ὀρθῶς δοξάζει
  - πάλιν ἀγνοεῖ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger extends the letter-and-syllable model to the soul, saying it can stand firm in truth about elements in some contexts yet fail to recognize the same things when transferred into long and difficult complexes.
textual_basis: "At 278d, the Stranger applies the model to the soul's handling of the elements of all things: it rightly opines in some combinations, but when the same things are moved into longer and less easy syllable-like complexes, it fails to recognize them again."
limits: This records the cognitive analogy. It does not claim that the passage gives a full theory of elements or that the model is limited to literal letters.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0127
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 278a-278b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  - παραδείγματα
  - παραδείγματα οὕτω γιγνόμενα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger gives a procedural definition of how a paradeigma comes into being: when things correctly opined are set alongside unknown things and their shared similarity and nature are shown through comparison, they "thus become paradigms."
textual_basis: |
  At 278b the Stranger states that after true opinions are placed alongside unknown things and the similarity is demonstrated, "thus becoming paradigms" (paradeigmata houto gignomena), defining the genesis of a paradigm as the outcome of a comparative procedure rather than as a pre-existing object.
limits: |
  This observation records the definition at this point in the dialogue; it does not assess whether this account is consistent with other uses of paradeigma elsewhere in the Statesman or in other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0128

greek_terms:
  - στοιχείων
  - συλλαβαῖς
  - παραδείγματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger uses the letter-and-syllable model to explain how generated paradigms let each element be distinguished from others across all syllabic combinations.
textual_basis: At 278a-278b, the Stranger describes leading learners from things correctly opined to unknown things, comparing both until paradigms are generated; those paradigms then make each element in every syllable distinct from the others.
limits: This records the local explanatory analogy. It does not evaluate whether the paradigm procedure succeeds or restate the whole paradigm-definition sequence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0129
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 278a-278b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 278a-278b
  start_marker: 278a
  end_marker: 278b
  start_char: 42904
  end_char: 43569
  text_sha256: 8d5f9bc682614d3328c89fed69c76ab9d628f6b1ef2c28006a632825dbfe55eb
greek_terms:
  - ὁμοιότητα
  - φύσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger lays out a multi-step procedure for using paradigms to teach: first lead someone back to what they already correctly believe, set those beliefs alongside what is unknown, compare them to reveal shared similarity and nature, and continue until the unknown is illuminated by the known.
textual_basis: |
  At 278a-278b the Stranger, responding to Young Socrates, proposes a method (anagein proton... tithenai para... paraballontas endeiknunai) for bringing someone from familiar correct opinions to knowledge of unfamiliar matters. He frames this as the easiest and finest way (rhaston kai kalliston) to lead people toward what is not yet known.
limits: |
  This observation identifies a procedural announcement within the dialogue's turn geometry. It does not evaluate the method's philosophical adequacy or its relationship to other Platonic methodological passages such as recollection.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0130
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 278e-279a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 278e-279a
  start_marker: 278e
  end_marker: 279a
  start_char: 44318
  end_char: 45227
  text_sha256: dfa56759fd3a9b3de1d84e59ec8eea338ad61fcc9f9153ca228f4aa4c9c77d5b
greek_terms:
  - παραδείγματος
  - εἶδος
  - τέχνῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Visitor explicitly states the methodological procedure of using a smaller paradigm to illuminate a larger target, moving from lesser examples to the kingly form in order to recognize the political art, with the aim of reaching waking knowledge rather than a dream-like state.
textual_basis: At 278e-279a the Visitor describes first attempting to see the nature of a whole paradigm in small part through another paradigm, then moving from smaller things to the greatest case — the kingly form (eidos) — through a paradigm to recognize the care of the city by art (techne).
limits: This observation records the explicit methodological statement but does not assess whether the method is successfully executed or evaluate the paradigm chosen.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 278e the Visitor states that the paradigm procedure is undertaken so that it becomes waking reality (hupar) instead of a dream (oneiratos) for the inquirers.
limits: This observation records the simile only; it does not claim the passage is mythic or assess whether the dream/waking contrast maps onto a specific epistemological doctrine elsewhere.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0132

english_gloss: ""
observation: The Visitor announces a return to the previous argument, recapitulating the claim that a paradigm is needed to separate the genuine kingly kind from its countless rivals who dispute its care of cities.
textual_basis: At 279a the Visitor says the previous argument must be taken up again (palin de ton emprosthe logon analepteon) and rehearses the earlier conclusion that, since countless people dispute the kingly kind's care of cities, they must all be separated off to leave only that one, for which a paradigm is needed.
limits: This observation records the procedural recapitulation; it does not evaluate whether the restarted argument succeeds or how it differs from the earlier formulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0133
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 280c-280d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 280c-280d
  start_marker: 280c
  end_marker: 280d
  start_char: 47567
  end_char: 48330
  text_sha256: 909cba96283f2f8a6432b6402f13b92ba9c38bbd2864ce6c157e6afb7296634b
greek_terms:
  - ἀφείλομεν
  - ἀφωρισάμεθα
  - ἀπετεμόμεθα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 280c-280d the Eleatic Stranger rapidly recapitulates a series of craft-exclusions already made in the diairetic hunt for the statesman: linen and cord crafts (280c), felt-making and stitched leatherwork (280c), whole-hide shelter-crafts (280c-280d), barrier-crafts against theft and violence (280d), and weapon-making (280d). Each is marked by a past-tense separation verb (apheilomen, aphorisametha, apetemometha)."
textual_basis: The verbs apheilomen (we removed), aphorisametha (we demarcated), and apetemometha (we cut off) at 280c-280d confirm that these craft-domains have already been separated off in the ongoing diairetic procedure.
limits: This observation notes the recapitulation and the diairetic exclusion vocabulary but does not analyze whether any of these exclusions might be revised later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0134
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 280c-280d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 280c-280d
  start_marker: 280c
  end_marker: 280d
  start_char: 47567
  end_char: 48330
  text_sha256: 909cba96283f2f8a6432b6402f13b92ba9c38bbd2864ce6c157e6afb7296634b
greek_terms:
  - σκεπασμάτων
  - στεγασμάτων
  - φραγμάτων
  - προβληματουργικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 280c-280d the Stranger catalogues excluded covering-crafts by function: whole-hide coverings (dermatourgike), roof-coverings (stegasmata) in building and carpentry, barrier-crafts (phragmata) against theft and violence including lid-making and door-fittings, and weapon-making as a segment of projectile-craft (problematourgike). These are grouped as sub-technai of a broad covering/protection domain."
textual_basis: The passage clusters several low-prestige crafts (leatherwork, carpentry, barrier-work, weapon-making) under the functional rubric of covering (skepasmata, stegasmata) and protection (phragmata, probolematourgike), exemplifying the diairetic practice of abstracting a common function across humble crafts.
limits: This observation does not assert that these craft analogies map onto statesmanship; it only notes the functional grouping of humble crafts in the diairetic procedure.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0135
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 280a-280b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 280a-280b
  start_marker: 280a
  end_marker: 280b

  end_char: 47567
  text_sha256: 65f4b55ff65ce613a54de4321e8844fe6cc429747b2920c06d75c07bb2d0ef31
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὴν
  - πολιτικῆς
  - ὑφαντικήν
  - ἱματιουργικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger says that weaving (hyphantike) differs from cloak-making (himatiourgike) only in name, just as earlier the kingly art (basilike) differed from statesmanship (politike) only in name (280a).
textual_basis: "The Stranger explicitly constructs the parallel: 'let us also call this art himatiourgike ... and let us say that hyphantike, insofar as it was the greatest part in the working of cloaks, differs in no way except in name from this himatiourgike, just as there too the kingly art differed from statesmanship' (280a). The analogy maps the part-whole name relationship in weaving onto the same relationship earlier established for statesmanship."
limits: The observation records the explicit analogical mapping but does not assess whether the analogy holds or how it functions in the larger argument. The passage does not elaborate what substantive differences, if any, the name-only distinction conceals.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0136
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 280a-280b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 280a-280b
  start_marker: 280a
  end_marker: 280b
  start_char: 46747
  end_char: 47567
  text_sha256: 65f4b55ff65ce613a54de4321e8844fe6cc429747b2920c06d75c07bb2d0ef31
greek_terms:
  - συνεργῶν
  - συγγενῶν
  - ἐπανιτέον
  - στρωμάτων
  - περιβολῇ
  - ὑποβολῇ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger halts the definition of weaving at 280a-b: someone might think it sufficient, but closely cooperating arts (synergoi) have not yet been distinguished from kindred arts (syngeneis), so he initiates a procedural return (epaniteon) to redo a prior division concerning bedding (stromata)."
textual_basis: "At 280a-b the Stranger says: 'someone might think the weaving of cloaks thus described has been sufficiently stated, not being able to notice that it has not yet been distinguished from the closely cooperating arts, though it has been separated from many other kindred arts.' When Young Socrates asks which kindred arts, the Stranger says he must go back (epaniteon) starting from the end, recalling the division of bedding composition by wrapping (peribole) and underlaying (hypobole)."
limits: The observation records a definitional halt and procedural reset, not a refutation or aporia. The Stranger identifies an incomplete distinction, not a contradiction. The passage does not resolve whether the reset will succeed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0137
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 279d-279e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 279d-279e
  start_marker: 279d
  end_marker: 279e
  start_char: 46018
  end_char: 46747
  text_sha256: 65f5dc6c118cc49717fadc588290265094f96231a0fbc7a2c1922954c8283584
greek_terms:
  - προβλήματα
  - ὁπλίσματα
  - φράγματα
  - παραπετάσματα
  - ἀλεξητήρια
  - στεγάσματα
  - σκεπάσματα
  - ὑποπετάσματα
  - περικαλύμματα
  - ἱμάτια
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 279d-279e, the Eleatic Stranger applies the method of dichotomous division to protective coverings (problemata), producing a nested branching tree that descends through multiple levels before arriving at the name himatia for self-bound woven garments.
textual_basis: "The text traces a chain: problemata split into offensive (hoplismata) and defensive (phragmata); defensive split into screens (parapetasmata) and weather-protectors (alexeteria); alexeteria into roofed shelters (stegasmata) and wraparound covers (skepasmata); skepasmata into underlays (hypopetasmata) and wraparounds (perikalymmata); perikalymmata into single-piece (holoschista) and composite (syntheta); composite into pierced/bored (treta) and bound-without-piercing (aneu treseos syndeta); unpierced into plant-fiber (neurina) and hair-based (trichina); hair-based into those glued with water and earth and those self-bound. The self-bound protective coverings are given the name himatia (279e)."
limits: This observation records only the formal structure of the division and its terminus. It does not evaluate whether the division is correct, complete, or deliberately parodic, nor does it assess the Eleatic Stranger's pedagogical intent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: After Young Socrates assents that the protective art of making wool coverings has been called weaving, the Visitor halts the progression at 280e-281a, states the account is incomplete, and pivots to distinguishing carding from weaving.
limits: The observation does not claim anything about why the division was incomplete or whether the omission was deliberate.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0141
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 280e-281a

greek_terms:
  - διαλυτική
  - συμπλοκή
  - ξαντικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Visitor corrects the earlier division of weaving by distinguishing carding as a dissolutive operation from weaving as an interweaving operation, using the rhetorical question whether carding should be called weaving to force Young Socrates to reject the conflation at 281a.
textual_basis: At 281a, the Visitor contrasts the dissolutive work of the carder with the interweaving of weaving, and asks whether one would dare call carding "weaving." Young Socrates rejects this, confirming that a distinction omitted from the earlier division must now be incorporated.
limits: This observation records a correction in the diairetic procedure without claiming that the earlier omission indicates a flaw in the division method as such.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0142
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 281b, the Eleatic Stranger asks whether fulling and mending crafts should be considered as providing no care of clothing, or whether all of them should be called forms of weaving. Young Socrates replies "not at all," thereby excluding these crafts from the definition of weaving.
textual_basis: "The Stranger poses a dichotomous question at 281b: should these crafts be set down as providing no care of clothing, or called weaving? Young Socrates' negative answer completes a division step that distinguishes weaving from other clothing-care crafts."
limits: This observation records a single diairetic step within the broader definition-by-division method; it does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or correct by the dialogue's own standards.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0143
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 281b-c, the Stranger predicts that the fulling and mending crafts will dispute with the power of weaving over the care and production of clothing, conceding the greatest part to weaving but claiming large parts for themselves.
textual_basis: The Stranger states at 281b that these crafts "will dispute" with the weaving craft's power over both the care and generation of clothing. He describes the resulting distribution as the greatest part going to weaving but large parts allotted to the competing crafts themselves.
limits: This observation captures the competitive scope-demarcation among crafts as dramatized by the Stranger. It does not evaluate whether such disputes reflect a genuine methodological problem or are merely a dialectical device.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0144
source_work: Statesman

  - συναιτίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 281c, the Stranger introduces a distinction between causes and co-causes: the crafts that produce tools for weaving are to be considered co-causes of every woven product, not primary causes."
textual_basis: The Stranger says at 281c that the crafts producing the tools through which the works of weaving are completed should be considered as "being co-causes" of every woven product. Young Socrates responds with "most correct," confirming the distinction.
limits: This observation records the introduction of the cause/co-cause distinction within the division of crafts. It does not evaluate whether the distinction is consistently maintained throughout the dialogue or whether it maps onto other causal frameworks in Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 279b the Stranger asks whether they should select weaving as a paradigm if they have nothing else ready at hand, and proposes taking only the part about woolen fabrics, saying that even this part might bear witness to what they are seeking — namely the statesman.
limits: This observation notes the proposal of weaving as a methodological paradigm. It does not assess whether the paradigm is adequate, nor does it claim the Stranger equates weaving with statecraft.

```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 279b-c the Stranger says that just as before they cut and divided each part of parts, so now they should do the same thing concerning weaving, and specifies doing so through brief means quickly covering everything before returning to what is now useful.
limits: This observation records the procedural announcement. It does not evaluate whether the subsequent division in fact follows the announced method, nor does it track how the method maps onto the earlier Sophist divisions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0148
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 281d-281e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 281d-281e
  start_marker: 281d
  end_marker: 281e
  start_char: 49893
  end_char: 50788
  text_sha256: 812d777503bd6f7093ca6a0a4044712505026f9c416fc14bb3f64def44793981
greek_terms:
  - συναίτιον
  - αἰτίαν
  - τέχνας
  - ὄργανα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger introduces a distinction between two kinds of craft (techne) concerning all things done: one is a contributing cause (synaitios) of production, the other is a cause (aitia) itself. Contributory crafts do not make the thing itself but prepare tools for the productive crafts; without them the work assigned to each craft could not be accomplished (281d-281e)."
textual_basis: At 281d the Stranger states he will examine two crafts (techne, plural) pertaining to all actions, then at 281e defines the first as those that do not produce the thing itself but prepare tools (organa) for the productive crafts, calling them synaitious (contributory causes), and the second as those that accomplish the thing itself, calling them aitias (causes).
limits: This observation records the classification schema as stated; it does not evaluate whether the distinction between contributory and proper causes holds across all craft domains. It does not address how this maps onto the statesman or kingly craft, which is the subject of the surrounding discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - στρεπτικόν
  - συμπλεκτικόν
english_gloss: twisting, intertwining
observation: Young Socrates tentatively checks his understanding of the Visitor's division, asking whether the twisting art pertains to warp-work.
textual_basis: At 282d, after the Visitor names the two parts of the combinative art, Young Socrates responds by asking whether he understands correctly, tentatively suggesting that the work concerning the warp is twisting — a qualified response that seeks confirmation rather than giving simple assent. The Visitor confirms and extends the point to the woof as well.
limits: This records the respondent's tentative engagement with the division; it does not assess whether his understanding is correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "The Stranger says that carding and part of the shuttle-work, and whatever separates things that are combined, belong to wool-working and are also parts of two great crafts: the combinative and separative at 282b."
```

```yaml

observation: At 282a the Stranger explicitly flags that he is subsuming a group of related crafts (fulling, mending, and all attendant care) under a single craft-name, the craft of fulling (knafeutikē), calling attention to the naming convention as part of the diairetic procedure.
textual_basis: The Stranger says it is reasonable to encompass this portion of the ornamental art and name it all by the craft of fulling at 282a, using the participle that marks the naming move explicitly.
limits: This records the explicit naming move but does not evaluate whether the subsumption is accurate or whether other naming conventions in the dialogue follow the same pattern.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0155
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 283b-283c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 283b-283c
  start_marker: 283b
  end_marker: 283c
  start_char: 53381
  end_char: 54190
  text_sha256: 7dad97394849f79e692271a5bc679a13086343c8e34cad862b29eed292e5ced6
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger retrospectively questions the roundabout procedure of the division method, asking why they did not answer directly that weaving is the interweaving of warp and woof but instead went around in a circle making many distinctions in vain (283b). Young Socrates defends the procedure, and the Stranger acknowledges this defense before launching into a new methodological account about excess and deficiency (283b-283c).
textual_basis: At 283b the Stranger asks why they did not answer immediately but instead went around in a circle making many distinctions in vain. Young Socrates responds that nothing seemed to have been said in vain (283b). The Stranger then transitions to a new topic about excess and deficiency (283c).
limits: This observation notes the retrospective methodological reflection and the transition it enables. It does not assess whether the Stranger truly regards the prior divisions as useless or whether this is a staged Socratic move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0156
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 283b-283c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  end_char: 54190
  text_sha256: 7dad97394849f79e692271a5bc679a13086343c8e34cad862b29eed292e5ced6
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger announces a programmatic examination of excess and deficiency as criteria for judging the appropriate length of discourse, framing this as an account that applies to all such cases (283c).
textual_basis: At 283c the Stranger declares that they should first examine all excess and deficiency, so that they may praise and blame according to reason the things said at greater length than needed and their opposites concerning such pastimes. He adds that this account properly concerns length, brevity, and all excess (283c).
limits: This observation records the methodological transition and the announced criteria. It does not evaluate whether the subsequent discussion of measurement fulfills this program, nor does it assess the philosophical adequacy of excess and deficiency as criteria.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0157

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger characterizes the tendency toward excessive length in dialectical procedure as a disease or ailment, applying medical vocabulary to a discursive practice (283b-283c).
textual_basis: At 283b-283c the Stranger refers to the roundabout, excessively long procedure as 'this kind of ailment,' saying it might recur often later, and offers to give an account concerning it. The medical term for disease is used to describe a methodological failing in discourse.
limits: This observation notes the medical analogy for discourse pathology. It does not claim that the Stranger is advancing a systematic theory of method-as-medicine, nor does it assess whether the subsequent discussion of measurement fulfills the therapeutic promise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0158
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 283e-284a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 283e-284a
  start_marker: 283e
  end_marker: 284a
  start_char: 54643
  end_char: 55615
  text_sha256: 65568e79c773682e52874284d3ee1e0e09d5b2a9085dc0809e85f04833473747
greek_terms:
  - διττὰς
  - οὐσίας
  - κρίσεις
  - πρὸς ἄλληλα
  - πρὸς τὸ μέτριον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger introduces a distinction between two kinds of measurement of great and small: one relative to each other (pros allela), the other relative to the due measure (pros to metrion). He calls these two essences and judgments (ousias kai kriseis) of great and small.
textual_basis: |
  At 283e the Stranger asks whether exceeding and being exceeded by the nature of the due measure in words and deeds really occurs, and in what the good and bad differ. He then explicitly states that these are two essences and judgments and that, contrary to what was said earlier, they are not only relative to each other but also relative to the due measure.
limits: |
  This observation records the explicit distinction drawn between two modes of measurement. It does not assess whether the distinction is tenable or how the due measure is to be determined.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0159

  - ὑφαντικὴν
  - μέτριον

  - ἔλαττον
  - μέτρον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger argues that allowing greater and smaller only in relation to each other would destroy the crafts, including the political art and weaving, because such crafts preserve measure by guarding excess and deficiency in practice.
textual_basis: The passage names the political art and weaving, says the argument would eliminate crafts and their works, and then states that these crafts produce good and fine results by preserving measure.
limits: This records the craft-dependence argument. It does not restate the prior twofold measurement distinction or the later comparison with another inquiry.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0162
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 282e-283a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 282e-283a
  start_marker: 282e
  end_marker: 283a
  start_char: 52469
  end_char: 53381
  text_sha256: 802d948186395406875b2bd85174a472bdf918c8f52b7a13253f0d2669fd7f52
greek_terms:
  - κρόκη
  - στημονονητική
  - ὑφαντική
english_gloss: warp, woof, warp-spinning art, woof-spinning art, weaving art
observation: The Eleatic Stranger partitions the weaver's material—first into warp and woof threads by their preparation method, then names the corresponding sub-crafts (warp-spinning art and woof-spinning art), and finally defines the overarching art (weaving) by its product (woven woolen garment).
textual_basis: At 282e the Stranger asks the Young Socrates to distinguish each part. He then states that the twisted, solid thread is called warp and the art directing it is the warp-spinning art; the loose, soft thread that is woven across the warp is called woof and its art the woof-spinning art (283a). The composite product of the straight-crossing weave is a woolen garment, and the art supervising this is named weaving. The whole passage follows the diairetic method of dividing a genus into species by discovering the defining sub-operations.
limits: This record notes the diairetic structure and the naming of sub-crafts. It does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or whether the Weaving paradigm is adequate for the political art.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0163
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 282e-283a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 282e-283a
  start_marker: 282e
  end_marker: 283a
  start_char: 52469
  end_char: 53381
  text_sha256: 802d948186395406875b2bd85174a472bdf918c8f52b7a13253f0d2669fd7f52
greek_terms:
  - συγκριτικῆς
  - εὐθυπλοκίᾳ
  - ἐσθῆτα ἐρεᾶν
  - ὑφαντική
english_gloss: combinatory art, straight-crossing plaiting, woolen garment, weaving art
observation: "The Stranger consolidates the preceding diairetic steps into a single definitional chain: the combined product of the cross-plaiting of warp and woof is the woven garment, and the art set over this product is weaving. The chain links material preparation, sub-craft names, and the comprehensive art name into one unified statement."
textual_basis: At 283a the Stranger declares that the part of the combinatory art in wool-working, when it produces a plaiting by straight-crossing of woof and warp, the whole plaiting is a woolen garment, and the art set over this is called weaving. This recapitulates the earlier distinctions (warp-art, woof-art) and ties them to the overarching weaver's art.
limits: This observation records the definitional consolidation within the paradigm. It does not argue that the political art is defined in the same way, nor does it assess the validity of the diairetic method.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ὀρθότατα
english_gloss: yes, correctly, most correctly
observation: The Young Socrates gives three successive assent markers to the Stranger's proposed distinctions and definition of weaving, signaling that the respondent accepts the diairetic moves without objection.
textual_basis: At 282e the Young Socrates answers with a simple assent to the initial example of carded wool; at 282e–283a he says 'correctly' to the warp/warp-art distinction and 'most correctly' to the woof/woof-art and final weaving definition. The dense series of affirmative responses shows the respondent following the Stranger's procedure step by step.
limits: This observation records the respondent's assent pattern. It does not claim that the Young Socrates fully understands the paradigm or that the definition is correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0165
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 284d-284e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  text_sha256: b2458a287c2693058fe46a0831c644e5aa6c5f5ea335788cff7d20ee8706965c
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 284e the Eleatic Stranger divides the art of measurement (metretike) into two parts. One part measures numbers, lengths, depths, widths, and speeds against their opposites; the other measures against the mean, the fitting, the opportune, the needful, and everything that has settled in the middle away from the extremes.
textual_basis: The Stranger states at 284e that they would divide metretike by cutting it in two (dicha temnontes), listing two distinct kinds of measurement with their respective objects. Young Socrates confirms both are large divisions differing greatly from each other.
limits: This records a single diairetic division step within the larger search for the statesman's art. It does not determine which kind of measurement belongs to the statesman, nor does it assess whether the division is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0166

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 284d the Eleatic Stranger asserts that all crafts (technai) must be understood as measuring greater and smaller not only relative to each other but also relative to the generation of the mean (to metrion).
textual_basis: The Stranger says the present argument (logos) shows that one must consider all crafts alike as measuring greater and smaller against each other and also against the generation of the mean, and that the existence of each kind of measurement depends on the other.
limits: This records a methodological claim that every craft involves measurement against a standard of the mean. It does not evaluate whether this claim holds for all crafts or whether it follows from the earlier argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The passage moves from elders returning toward childhood to dead bodies in the earth being reconstituted and revived, then completes the account by naming earthborn generation.
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 272a the narrative says all revived from earth, remembered nothing previous, received abundant fruit without farming from the self-yielding earth, and lived mostly naked and without bedding outdoors because the seasons were harmless.
limits: This records the contents of the mythic life-description. It does not judge whether the life is happier or whether the account is literal.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0171

  - βασιλικῆς ἐπιστήμης

  - πλέον

english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger says the inquiry into kingly knowledge becomes impassable if the political art is eliminated, and compares the present need to admit measurable excess and deficiency with the Sophist's forced admission of not-being.
textual_basis: At 284b he states that abolishing the political art would leave the subsequent search for kingly knowledge without a path, then asks whether greater and smaller must be compelled to become measurable not only against each other.
limits: This records a methodological dependence of the political inquiry on the measure argument. It does not prove the measure doctrine or analyze the Sophist parallel beyond the explicit comparison.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0173
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 285a

greek_terms:
  - μετρητικὴ
  - ἔντεχνα
  - εἴδη
  - διαιρουμένους
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger diagnoses a classificatory error: people collapse very different measured arts into one because they are not accustomed to examine by kinds, and they also fail to divide differences by parts."
textual_basis: At 285a he reports the view that measurement concerns all generated things, then criticizes the habit of immediately combining greatly different things as the same and, conversely, not dividing different things according to their parts.
limits: This records the methodological diagnosis. It does not name which later divisions will repair the error.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0174
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 285b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 285b
  start_marker: 285b
  end_marker: 285b
  start_char: 57838
  end_char: 58284
  text_sha256: 055b04a2609744d55d4f9038bdbf72a7db585c1e9026fbdde3cf78e6d384b2bd
greek_terms:
  - κοινωνίαν
  - διαφορὰς
  - εἴδεσι
  - ὁμοιότητος
  - γένους
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger states a paired rule for division and collection: after perceiving commonality in many things, one must see all differences in kinds, and after seeing many dissimilarities, one must enclose the related things within one likeness and genus."
textual_basis: "At 285b the rule is stated in both directions: do not stop at the many's commonality before seeing the differences lying in kinds, and do not stop at scattered dissimilarities before gathering all related items within a single likeness and genus."
limits: This records a methodological rule for inquiry. It does not apply the rule to a specific division inside this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0175
source_work: Statesman

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 285c

  end_char: 58694
  text_sha256: 3a0c05e4be14e80bed1755b82ea04e2f80b65adf28e3e5b3fd6dcb1f0eb455d4
greek_terms:
  - μετρητικῆς
  - γράμματα
  - συνουσίαν
  - ζητουμένων
english_gloss: ""
observation: After summarizing the two kinds of measurement, the Stranger announces a further account about the objects under inquiry and about the whole practice of spending time in such discussions.
textual_basis: At 285c he tells Young Socrates to remember the two discovered kinds of measurement, then proposes another account concerning both the things being sought and the whole sustained activity in these kinds of arguments.
limits: This is a transition into methodological reflection. The span begins the letters example but does not complete the analogy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0176
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 285d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 285d
  start_marker: 285d
  end_marker: 285d
  start_char: 58694
  end_char: 59178
  text_sha256: 52e7102de5c3506da5f9283cf117e8f238522a7d4e8f8b376fcb5d89c1dd2e54
greek_terms:
  - γραμματικωτέρῳ
  - πολιτικοῦ
  - διαλεκτικωτέροις
  - ὑφαντικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger declares that the inquiry into the statesman is pursued not merely for that target itself but to make the participants more dialectical about all things, just as letter-learning aims beyond one assigned word.
textual_basis: At 285d he asks whether letter instruction is for one assigned case or for becoming more grammatical about all cases, then asks the same about the statesman inquiry; Young Socrates answers that it is for becoming more dialectical about all.
limits: This records the stated training purpose of the inquiry. It does not show that the participants actually become more dialectical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0177
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 285e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 285e

  end_marker: 285e

  end_char: 59405
  text_sha256: fd52e589d895258344f9f0cf3192f4c07a265d43cf19727fb73edad6e2fdccad
greek_terms:
  - αἰσθηταί
  - ὁμοιότητες
  - λόγον
  - μεγίστοις
  - τιμιωτάτοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger contrasts easy cases, where perceptible likenesses can be shown to someone asking for an account, with the greatest and most honored things, for which the passage begins a contrasting claim.
textual_basis: At 285e he says some things have perceptible likenesses that are easy to learn and show when someone asks for an account, but then turns to the greatest and most honored things as a different class.
limits: The exact positive claim about the greatest things continues into the next span, so this record should stay limited to the contrast as introduced here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0178
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 286a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 286a
  start_marker: 286a
  end_marker: 286a
  start_char: 59405
  end_char: 59803
  text_sha256: f4f993834640bc297cba993e6b2927baefd941b1a17a9d7f753794e59dff9071
greek_terms:
  - εἴδωλον
  - αἰσθήσεών
  - λόγον
  - ἀσώματα
  - σαφῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger says the greatest incorporeal things lack a clear human-made image for sensory display and are shown clearly by account alone.
textual_basis: At 286a he denies that there is any clear image by which the soul of the inquirer can be satisfied through the senses, then states that the incorporeal things, being finest and greatest, are shown clearly only by account.
limits: This records the contrast between sensory showing and account-based showing. It does not identify the incorporeal objects beyond the general class named here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0179
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 286b-286c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 286b-286c
  start_marker: 286b
  end_marker: 286c
  start_char: 59803
  end_char: 60667
  text_sha256: 66ea2328196db89aeca0e144639236e25a3245fc31f39e489ce0dc9ce78cf1fc
greek_terms:
  - μελέτη
  - μακρολογίαν
  - ὑφαντικὴν
  - ἀνείλιξιν
  - μὴ ὄντος
  - μακρὰ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger recalls the participants' discomfort with lengthy discussions of weaving, cosmic unwinding, and the account of not-being, and says they reproached themselves over the risk of speaking needlessly and at length.
textual_basis: The passage lists the three extended discussions, identifies concern over their length, and completes the point by describing self-reproach over unnecessary and lengthy speech.
limits: This records the self-critique of discourse length. It does not decide whether the discussions were actually excessive and does not include the rule for future praise and blame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0180
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 286c-286d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 286c-286d
  start_marker: 286c
  end_marker: 286d
  start_char: 60263
  end_char: 61098
  text_sha256: c05a0eb7709aaf2b34a6d420478900f931427288f4f957fd30066cb6aa018552
greek_terms:
  - ψόγον
  - ἔπαινον
  - βραχύτητος
  - μήκους
  - πρέπον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger says future praise and blame of brevity and length should not judge lengths against one another, but should judge them by the relevant measure of fittingness.
textual_basis: The passage explicitly joins praise and blame with brevity and length, rejects comparison of lengths with each other, and names fittingness as the relevant standard.
limits: This record captures the negative rule and its immediate standard. It does not include the following ranking of method over speed, which is already accepted nearby.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0181
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 286d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 286d
  start_marker: 286d
  end_marker: 286d
  start_char: 60667
  end_char: 61098
  text_sha256: d76278b76ab11255dfb235b3fa6cf1bde05a30203b70109163189bc440dc1090
greek_terms:
  - μετρητικῆς
  - πρέπον
  - μέθοδον
  - εἴδη
  - διαιρεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger states the positive criterion for assessing discourse length: judge by the fitting, and honor the method of division by kinds more than speed in finding the proposed object."
textual_basis: At 286d he names the relevant part of measurement as the fitting, rejects pleasure in fitting length as primary, makes quick discovery of the proposed inquiry secondary, and gives first honor to the method that enables division by kinds.
limits: This records the methodological priority as stated. It does not prove that the following discourse satisfies that criterion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0182
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 286e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 286e
  start_marker: 286e
  end_marker: 286e
  start_char: 61098
  end_char: 61462
  text_sha256: 5305d80e9f8a451889b16e5db2e6591cbb0a8b104b8901c5390428eaf9a1f660
greek_terms:
  - παμμήκης
  - εὑρετικώτερον
  - βραχύτερος
  - μήκη
  - περιόδους
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger says a long account should be pursued without irritation if it makes the hearer more capable of discovery, and he begins to require critics of lengthy circular routes to do more than complain about length.
textual_basis: At 286e he says that even a very long account should be taken seriously if it makes the listener more inventive, and that the same holds for a shorter one; he then turns to the person who blames lengthy discussions and circular routes.
limits: The criterion for a legitimate criticism is completed in the next span, so this record should not treat the condition as fully stated here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0183
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 287a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 287a
  start_marker: 287a
  end_marker: 287a
  start_char: 61462
  end_char: 61840
  text_sha256: 6243b71f39e1fc72957f956ce623309d739a41315d9c0e5ff6047dc2acd7f291
greek_terms:
  - μακρὰ
  - βραχύτερα
  - διαλεκτικωτέρους
  - εὑρετικωτέρους
  - πολιτικὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger requires a critic of long discourse to show that a shorter version would have made the participants more dialectical and more capable of discovering beings by account; other praise and blame are to be ignored.
textual_basis: At 287a he says the critic must add a showing that shorter speeches would have made the companions more dialectical and more inventive in the account-based disclosure of beings, and that other kinds of praise and blame should not be heeded.
limits: This records a methodological standard for evaluating discourse. The return to the statesman begins at the end of the span but is not completed until the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0184
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 287b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 287b
  start_marker: 287b
  end_marker: 287b
  start_char: 61840
  end_char: 62240
  text_sha256: 5c839ce2f6a4fdb0d52e1542a86ae41e1668cf0c81a1093993bacc800391297f
greek_terms:
  - ὑφαντικῆς
  - παράδειγμα
  - βασιλεὺς
  - συναιτίων
  - αἰτίων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Returning to the statesman with the weaving paradigm, the Stranger says the king has been separated from herd-related arts and that the remaining city arts must first be divided into co-causes and causes.
textual_basis: At 287b he says they should go back to the statesman while carrying the weaving paradigm, then states that the king is separated from herd-related arts and that the remaining city arts, of co-causes and causes, must first be divided from each other.
limits: This records the reapplication of the cause/co-cause distinction to the political inquiry. It does not perform the division or identify all city arts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0185
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 287c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 287c
  start_marker: 287c
  end_marker: 287c
  start_char: 62240
  end_char: 62674
  text_sha256: 62ffa3f4b2795b056ae32d7afb650bb1f18fcb63373e30ce2cf6e85e264e8264
greek_terms:
  - δίχα
  - κατὰ μέλη
  - ἱερεῖον
  - συναιτίους
english_gloss: ""
observation: Because the city arts are difficult to cut in two, the Stranger proposes dividing them by limbs like a sacrificial animal and cutting always into the nearest possible number, then recalls the earlier treatment of weaving instruments as co-causes.
textual_basis: At 287c he states that cutting the relevant arts in two is difficult, proposes division by limbs like a sacrificial animal, and says one must always cut into the closest possible number; he then cites the prior classification of weaving instruments as co-causes.
limits: This records the proposed alternative to strict dichotomy. It does not complete the political division.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0186
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 287d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 287d
  start_marker: 287d
  end_marker: 287d
  start_char: 62674
  end_char: 63089
  text_sha256: a06236ba7a0f176ef6cad935ce81a2105094ec905f7950baebfc33ca85bfb648
greek_terms:
  - συναιτίους
  - πόλις
  - πολιτική
  - βασιλικῆς
  - τέχνης
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger classifies all crafts that produce civic instruments as co-causes: without them city and political art could not exist, but none of them is to be counted as the work of the kingly art."
textual_basis: At 287d he says every craft that makes a small or large instrument in the city should be set down as a co-cause, because without them no city or political art could arise, while none of these will be assigned as the work of the kingly art.
limits: This records the co-cause classification of instrument-producing civic crafts. It does not yet separate the kingly art itself from every other remaining kind.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0187
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 287e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 287e

  end_marker: 287e

  end_char: 63512
  text_sha256: d4b18e5f92724253d3e1b5fadd8f410eb512c0699d1572dfe7de95590635d8a6
greek_terms:
  - ἀγγεῖον
  - γενέσεως
  - σωτηρίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger distinguishes a class of possessions made not as an instrument for producing something but for preserving what has already been produced, then introduces the vessel as the example of that function.
textual_basis: At 287e the Stranger says the class is not set up for the cause of generation like an instrument, but for the preservation of the made thing. He then names the kind that serves dry, wet, fired, and unfired contents as a vessel.
limits: This records the production-preservation distinction inside the civic possession inventory. The marker does not complete the later statement about this class's relation to the sought political knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0188
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 288a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 288a
  start_marker: 288a
  end_marker: 288a
  start_char: 63512
  end_char: 63944
  text_sha256: e70417c553a4eac3308ab90a93e55d3721f9f91f509bb614d5fa7b80c33d2b29
greek_terms:
  - ὄχημα
  - θᾶκος
  - πολιτικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger marks vehicles as a third possession class, defined by their use as seats or supports, and assigns their production to carpentry, pottery, and bronze-working rather than to political craft.
textual_basis: At 288a the Stranger names the third class as a vehicle, describes it as something made for a seat or support, and explicitly says it is not much the work of political craft but much more of other productive crafts.
limits: This record identifies the exclusion of vehicle-making from political craft. It does not claim that vehicle-making is irrelevant to the city apart from this scope demarcation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0189
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 288b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 288b
  start_marker: 288b
  end_marker: 288b

  end_char: 64370
  text_sha256: b9de75f945f28fdf04b83ae7aeda2bcd3c801063a4083a5c895b222190ebc933
greek_terms:
  - πρόβλημα
  - ἐσθής
  - τείχη
  - πολιτικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger groups clothing, most arms, walls, and other protective coverings under the single class of protective barrier and assigns most of this work to building and weaving rather than to political craft.
textual_basis: At 288b he lists clothing, many weapons, earthen and stone walls, and many other items made for projection or protection, then says the whole would be more correctly regarded as the work of building and weaving than of political craft.
limits: The observation records a craft-scope exclusion for protective products. It does not settle whether the political art later uses or commands such products.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0190
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 288c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 288c
  start_marker: 288c
  end_marker: 288c

  end_char: 64774
  text_sha256: b167a7efccf20b1b0865c27df996310d68d38337a41db72e4ca15d3374768f39
greek_terms:
  - κόσμον
  - γραφικὴν
  - μουσικῇ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger gives ornament, painting, and music-assisted imitations one common name, plaything, because their products are made for pleasure rather than for serious use.
textual_basis: At 288c he asks whether the fifth class should include ornament, painting, and imitations completed with the aid of music, says they are made only for pleasure, and then states that one name, plaything, fits all of them because they are done for play rather than seriousness.
limits: This records the naming move within the division of possessions. It does not make a general claim about all mimetic or musical activity outside this classification.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0191
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 288d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  - σώματα
  - χρυσόν
  - ἄργυρον
  - δρυοτομικὴ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger begins a sixth possession class consisting of bodily materials supplied to the previously named crafts, listing mined metals and timber or cut plant material as examples.
textual_basis: At 288d he asks whether the class that provides bodies from which and in which the named crafts produce should be placed sixth, then lists gold, silver, mined materials, and wood or cut material supplied to carpentry and plaiting.
limits: The record is limited to the material-supply function begun at 288d. The formal name and royal-art exclusion of this class are completed in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0192
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 288e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 288e
  start_marker: 288e
  end_marker: 288e

  end_char: 65618

greek_terms:
  - πρωτογενὲς
  - ἀσύνθετον
  - βασιλικῆς ἐπιστήμης
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger completes the raw-material class by naming it primary and unsynthesized human possession, and he explicitly denies that it is a work of royal knowledge.
textual_basis: At 288e he adds hides, bark, cork, papyrus, and bindings as materials for composite products, then says the whole class is primary, unsynthesized possession for humans and not at all a work of royal knowledge.
limits: This records only the exclusion of raw material production from royal knowledge. It does not claim that royal knowledge cannot direct or use such materials.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0193
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 289a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 289a
```
