```yaml
greek_terms:
  - τροφόν
  - γεωργικῇ
  - θηρευτικῇ
  - πολιτικῇ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger makes nourishment the seventh possession class and assigns it to farming, hunting, gymnastics, medicine, and cooking more properly than to political craft.
textual_basis: At 289a he describes the acquisition of food and bodily mixtures that serve a bodily power, names the whole class nourishment, and says it should be assigned more correctly to the listed care and production crafts than to political craft.
limits: This record concerns the classification of nourishment among possessions. It does not evaluate whether political craft later coordinates the crafts that handle nourishment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0194
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 289b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 289b
  start_marker: 289b
  end_marker: 289b
  start_char: 66048
  end_char: 66483
  text_sha256: fd5c721fefa0b472dde1b5b150e7366d1ac4a72671bb06817114895ed18bba04
greek_terms:
  - πρωτογενὲς
  - ὄργανον
  - ἀγγεῖον
  - θρέμμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger recapitulates the possession inventory as seven genera and folds minor cases such as coinage, seals, and every stamp into the preceding classes rather than treating them as a major separate genus.
textual_basis: At 289b he lists the seven classes as primary material, instrument, vessel, vehicle, protective covering, plaything, and nourishment, then says omitted cases such as coinage, seals, and marks can be fitted into some of these classes, even if somewhat forcibly.
limits: This records a consolidation of the classification chain. It does not claim that the inventory is philosophically exhaustive beyond the Stranger's own qualified statement.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0195
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 289c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 289c
  start_marker: 289c
  end_marker: 289c
  start_char: 66483
  end_char: 66918
  text_sha256: e5cfa61154545190a318e2e01d465d6328983b865e7f08a469290ee3161d6d9b
greek_terms:
  - ἀγελαιοτροφικὴ
  - δούλων
  - ὑπηρετῶν
  - συναίτιοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger leaves slaves and servants as the remaining field to examine after the herd-rearing division and the separation of co-causes, expecting the rivals to the king to become visible there.
textual_basis: The passage says the prior herd-rearing division has taken in the tame-animal field except slaves, then identifies slaves and servants as what remains while setting aside the other co-causes.
limits: This records the narrowing of the candidate field. It does not identify the purchased-slave group or the later free-service groups.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0196
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 289d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 289d
  start_marker: 289d
  end_marker: 289d
  start_char: 66918
  end_char: 67285
  text_sha256: bd047e34e92cb09220e082c25c2b5a42910f460325c963b2215e9c953225d868
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικῆς
  - πολιτικῆς
  - ὑπηρέτας
  - ὠνητούς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Rejected repair: this span only transitions from separated co-causes to a closer examination of the remaining servants and begins to name purchased persons."
textual_basis: The marker says the remaining candidates should be examined more closely and starts to introduce the purchased group, but the explicit exclusion of slaves from royal craft is completed in the next accepted record.
limits: No accepted replacement is added because the transition-only content is already bracketed by the accepted remaining-field and slave-exclusion records.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0197
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 289e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 289e
  start_marker: 289e
  end_marker: 289e
  start_char: 67285
  end_char: 67723
  text_sha256: 4e9a7694470dad268cd848b000ccbd00d65a08394534f458cf683942dce26f47
greek_terms:
  - δούλους
  - ἐλευθέρων
  - ἀργυραμοιβούς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger distinguishes slaves, who make no claim to royal craft, from free people who voluntarily put themselves into service by moving and exchanging the products of farming and other crafts.
textual_basis: At 289e he says purchased people may unquestionably be called slaves and are least of all claiming royal craft. He then turns to free persons who choose service, describing those who convey and equalize products in markets and between cities by sea and land, including money-changers and merchants.
limits: The observation records the social and occupational types used in the division. It does not make a historical claim about actual economic classes outside the passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0198
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 290a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 290a
  start_marker: 290a
  end_marker: 290a
  start_char: 67723
  end_char: 68099
  text_sha256: 3fb6e260a5b97e93d2d9e7c483a4f0e4dde2e86aaa1a80d5ed76d3c69e38be10
greek_terms:
  - ναυκλήρους
  - καπήλους
  - μισθωτοὺς
  - θῆτας
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger allows that traders might dispute over commercial expertise, but he excludes wage workers and day laborers from any claim to royal craft and then asks about another group of attendants.
textual_basis: At 290a Young Socrates grants that merchants, shipowners, and retailers might perhaps dispute over the craft of commerce. The Stranger then says wage workers and day laborers, who are most ready to serve everyone, will never be found claiming royal craft, before asking about those who perform another kind of service.
limits: This records the stepwise narrowing of possible political-art claimants. It does not define the further attendants introduced at the end of the marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ὑπηρέτας
  - ἄρχοντας

observation: The Stranger tests heralds, literate specialists, and administrative workers around offices, and Young Socrates classifies them as servants rather than rulers in cities.
textual_basis: At 290b the Stranger names the heraldic class, those who become skilled in letters after repeated service, and others highly competent around offices. Young Socrates replies that they are servants, not the rulers themselves in cities.
limits: This observation records the servant-ruler distinction for administrative roles. It does not identify the later serious disputants over political craft.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0200
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  - μαντικὴν
  - διακόνου
  - ἑρμηνευταὶ
  - ἱερέων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger brings diviners and priests into the remaining comparison, treating divination as a ministering share of knowledge because diviners are regarded as interpreters from gods to humans.
textual_basis: At 290c he says the inquiry should come closer to those not yet tested, names those concerned with divination as having a ministering part of knowledge, and explains that they are considered interpreters from gods to humans. He then introduces the class of priests.
limits: This record identifies the professional types and the stated ministerial role of divination. It does not yet state the full priestly exchange function, which follows in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0201

greek_terms:

  - θυσιῶν
  - εὐχαῖς
  - διακόνου τέχνης
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger presents the priestly class as skilled in giving human gifts to gods through sacrifices and asking goods from gods through prayers, classifying both actions as parts of a serving craft.
textual_basis: The passage introduces the priestly class, describes the two directions of exchange, and then classifies both as parts of service.
limits: This records the assigned priestly service function. It does not identify priesthood with political craft or include the later examples of priestly status attached to ruling offices.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0202
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  - ἀρχαῖς
  - ἀρχαίων θυσιῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger gives civic examples in which priestly function attaches to high office, including Egyptian kingship and Greek offices responsible for major sacrifices.
textual_basis: The passage says Egyptian kings may not rule apart from priesthood, then adds Greek cases where major sacrifices are assigned to major offices, including the local lot-drawn king's ancestral sacrifices.
limits: This records examples used for comparison. It does not prove that priesthood is the same art as statesmanship.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0203
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  - κληρωτοὺς βασιλέας
  - ἱερέας
  - ὑπηρέτας
  - πάμφυλόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger announces that the next examination must include lot-drawn kings who are also priests, their servants, and another large crowd that has become visible only after the earlier separations.
textual_basis: At 291a he says these lot-drawn kings and priests, their servants, and some other very numerous crowd must be examined, now that the preceding groups have been set apart. He then describes this additional group as strange and many-formed.
limits: This record marks the procedural introduction of the next candidates. It does not yet identify the strange crowd, which the Stranger does in the following markers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0204
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 291a-291b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  - Κενταύροις
  - Σατύροις
  - ἰδέας
  - δύναμιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger characterizes the remaining political crowd as many-formed, comparing its members to mixed or shifting types and saying they quickly exchange forms and powers with one another.
textual_basis: The passage first introduces a very numerous and many-formed crowd, then gives a sequence of type-comparisons and states that their forms and powers change rapidly into one another.
limits: This records the typological imagery. It does not identify the crowd with the sophistic rival, which is stated in the next accepted record.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0205
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 291c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 291c
  start_marker: 291c
  end_marker: 291c
  start_char: 70542
  end_char: 70931
  text_sha256: fcd781d99a657fdb2bd8467b7511fa851039e4e2ac8e779943dc2b11653b467a
greek_terms:
  - σοφιστῶν
  - γόητα
  - πολιτικῶν
  - βασιλικῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger identifies the strange political crowd as the greatest magician among sophists and says this figure must be separated from genuine politicians and kings if the sought object is to be seen clearly.
textual_basis: At 291c he says he has suddenly recognized the chorus around city affairs, calls it the greatest magician of all sophists and most experienced in this craft, and states that, though difficult, it must be removed from the truly existing political and kingly figures in order to see the object being sought.
limits: This records the explicit exclusion of the sophistic rival from the political and kingly target. It does not explain the later criteria by which the separation will be made.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0206
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 291d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 291d
  start_marker: 291d
  end_marker: 291d
  start_char: 70931
  end_char: 71324
  text_sha256: dc745812a713b7817e730050439d184c48bc76e8c06304371f3756fe4055b339
greek_terms:
  - μοναρχία
  - δυναστείαν
  - δημοκρατία
  - πολιτείας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger names three constitutional forms: monarchy, rule by the few, and democracy, then asks whether these three somehow become five by producing two further names from themselves."
textual_basis: At 291d he asks whether monarchy is one of the political offices, next names the rule of the few, and third names the rule of the multitude called democracy. He then asks whether the three become five by generating two additional names from themselves.
limits: This records the opening of the constitutional-form classification. The two further names are not supplied within this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0207
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 291e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 291e
  start_marker: 291e
  end_marker: 291e
  start_char: 71324
  end_char: 71758
  text_sha256: 87b33a9f836d2fd7856e895f895fc34aac3405effb46a6d44c4ee2a62892dbc5
greek_terms:
  - βίαιόν
  - ἑκούσιον
  - τυραννίδι
  - βασιλικῇ
  - ἀριστοκρατίᾳ
  - ὀλιγαρχίᾳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger explains how the three constitutional forms are expanded by paired names: monarchy is divided into tyranny and kingship, while rule by the few is divided into aristocracy and oligarchy."
textual_basis: At 291e he says the forms are being considered by force and willingness, poverty and wealth, law and lawlessness. On that basis monarchy provides two names, tyranny and kingship, and the city ruled by the few is called aristocracy and oligarchy.
limits: This records the naming of constitutional forms by paired contrasts. The marker does not complete the treatment of democracy, which continues into the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0208
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 292a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 292a
  start_marker: 292a
  end_marker: 292a
  start_char: 71758
  end_char: 72170
  text_sha256: 6c0cf4b7d64743011c2f8c826ddead47571155ff658115049d86fb3394ceaf26
greek_terms:
  - πολιτειῶν
  - ὅροις
  - βιαίῳ
  - ἑκουσίῳ
  - γραμμάτων
  - νόμων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger asks whether any constitution is correctly bounded by the familiar external criteria of one, few, or many rulers; wealth or poverty; force or willingness; and written law or lawlessness.
textual_basis: The passage first notes that democracy keeps the same name despite variations in force, willingness, and law observance, then asks whether any constitution can be correctly defined by the listed boundaries.
limits: This records the criteria being put under question. It does not state the replacement criterion, which is introduced later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0209
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 292b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 292b
  start_marker: 292b
  end_marker: 292b
  start_char: 72170
  end_char: 72557
  text_sha256: ca30812942bece1945691247acc83bcd2e51cd753ee7f078e4589337c809cb54
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὴν ἀρχὴν
  - ἐπιστημῶν
  - κριτικὴν
  - ἐπιστατικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger restarts the inquiry by asking whether they will remain with the earlier claim that royal rule is a kind of knowledge, specifically a judging and directive kind selected from the other sciences.
textual_basis: At 292b he tells Young Socrates to follow more clearly and asks whether they will stand by or disagree with what was first said. He then recalls that they said royal rule was one of the sciences, and not all sciences but a judging and directive one.
limits: This is a procedural recapitulation of earlier divisions. The marker only begins the renewed classification and does not complete the application to political constitutions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0210
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 292c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 292c
  start_marker: 292c
  end_marker: 292c
  start_char: 72557
  end_char: 72971
  text_sha256: 3748dae251e182de3d402f5541ac5398937fe1db660a84bc3acd9fe9fad8465e
greek_terms:
  - ζῴοις
  - ἐπιστήμης
  - ὅρον
  - πενίαν
  - πλοῦτον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger states that the proper boundary for the political forms should not be number of rulers, willingness or unwillingness, poverty, or wealth, but some kind of knowledge.
textual_basis: At 292c he says the inquiry has advanced by dividing directive knowledge over lifeless works and living things, without forgetting knowledge but without yet defining exactly which knowledge. He then asks whether the boundary should be neither few nor many, neither willing nor unwilling, neither poverty nor wealth, but knowledge if they follow the earlier account.
limits: The observation records the announced replacement criterion. This marker does not yet identify which constitution, if any, actually contains that knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0211
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 292d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 292d
  start_marker: 292d
  end_marker: 292d
  start_char: 72971
  end_char: 73400
  text_sha256: cc77b8f95a8407c920738993b6bcf914862472084647bafa5ad84507ba4cd0d1
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμη
  - ἀνθρώπων ἀρχῆς
  - φρονίμου βασιλέως
  - προσποιοῦνται
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger says the inquiry must find where knowledge of ruling human beings occurs, so that the pretenders who claim to be political and persuade many can be removed from the prudent king.
textual_basis: "At 292d he calls knowledge concerning rule over humans nearly the hardest and greatest to acquire. He says they must see it in order to determine who must be taken away from the prudent king: those who pretend to be political and persuade many but are not so at all."
limits: This records the exclusion task announced for the next argument. It does not identify the pretenders by constitutional name within this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0212
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 292e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 292e
  start_marker: 292e
  end_marker: 292e
  start_char: 73400
  end_char: 73872
  text_sha256: 42194ade2be2c963a418df6d7bc89bd53f84a50ce9f75d3a8fed4a6a698b30b2
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμην
  - πεττευταὶ
  - βασιλῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger and Young Socrates test whether political knowledge could belong to a multitude or to dozens in a city, and Young Socrates rejects this by comparing such a number with the scarcity of top game players.
textual_basis: At 292e the Stranger asks whether the multitude in a city can possess the knowledge, and then whether one hundred or fifty in a city of a thousand could possess it adequately. Young Socrates replies that this would make it the easiest of all arts, since even that many top game players would not be found among a thousand Greeks, much less kings.
limits: The comparison is used to mark rarity of expertise. It does not prove the full identity of political knowledge or specify how many true possessors there are.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0213
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 293a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 293a

  end_marker: 293a

  end_char: 74291
  text_sha256: b1d4087d09d26686619c611d72ec594c34c2f3a31e1c0a6caada617088a94cc5
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὸν
  - ὀρθὴν ἀρχὴν
  - ὀλίγους
  - ἑκόντων
  - ἀκόντων
  - γράμματα
  - τέχνην
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger locates correct rule with one, two, or very few rulers, and classifies those rulers by art rather than by consent, written rules, or wealth.
textual_basis: Young Socrates recalls the royal classification, and the Stranger says correct rule should be sought around one, two, or very few. The same marker says such rulers should be counted as ruling by art whether subjects consent or not, whether rules are written or unwritten, and whether the rulers are rich or poor.
limits: This record stops before the medical analogy that begins after the marker and is handled by the following accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0214
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 293b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 293b
  start_marker: 293b
  end_marker: 293b
  start_char: 74291
  end_char: 74684
  text_sha256: ab00473af10d941eeb777ef88e927690da17c1be80be687683a908096e4d1cfc
greek_terms:
  - τέχνῃ
  - καθαίροντες
  - ἀγαθῷ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger uses doctors as the analogue for expert rule: they remain doctors whether they treat willing or unwilling patients, with cutting or burning, with or without written rules, and whether rich or poor, as long as they supervise by art for bodily good."
textual_basis: At 293b he says doctors are still called doctors whether they treat us willingly or unwillingly, by cutting, burning, or other painful procedures, according to writings or without writings, poor or rich. The condition is that they supervise by art and act for the good of bodies, purging, reducing, or increasing.
limits: This observation records the medical analogy as a criterion for expert status. It does not claim that political rule and medicine are identical in every respect.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0215
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 293c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 293c
  start_marker: 293c
  end_marker: 293c
  start_char: 74684
  end_char: 75099
  text_sha256: ccee354c301c1b07f314d5562bc85b531bb02642b9cd989452b2b929dc17fefb
greek_terms:
  - ὅρον
  - ἰατρικῆς
  - ἐπιστήμονας
  - νόμων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger states that the same criterion applies to constitutions: the only especially correct constitution is the one in which rulers are truly knowledgeable rather than merely seeming to be."
textual_basis: At 293c he concludes that saving what is treated by making it better from worse is the only correct boundary of medicine and any other rule. He then applies this to constitutions, saying the only correct constitution is the one where rulers are found to be truly knowledgeable and not merely seeming so, whether they rule according to laws or without laws.
limits: This records the criterion for correctness stated in the passage. It does not yet include the later examples of killing, exile, colonization, or citizen-importation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0216
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 293d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 293d
  start_marker: 293d
  end_marker: 293d
  start_char: 75099
  end_char: 75555
  text_sha256: e724d5ba5d0e3c98e2638277c697e7ea5f33056bd9048e6d84367e73788fc127
greek_terms:
  - καθαίρωσιν
  - ἀποικίας
  - σμήνη μελιττῶν
  - ἐπιστήμῃ
  - δικαίῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger extends the treatment analogy to civic actions, saying that even killing, exile, reducing a city through colonies, or increasing it by new citizens can belong to correct rule if done with knowledge and justice for preservation and improvement.
textual_basis: At 293d he says none of the external conditions counts for correctness. He then gives examples of purging the city by killing or exile, reducing it by sending colonies like swarms of bees, or increasing it by importing citizens, as long as rulers use knowledge and justice and make the city better from worse as far as they can.
limits: The record states the Stranger's criterion and examples. It does not endorse the civic actions or infer how they would be judged outside the stated condition of knowledge and justice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0217
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 293e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 293e
  start_marker: 293e
  end_marker: 293e
  start_char: 75555
  end_char: 75996
  text_sha256: e48f7636e3a801c62743e3f0760615a36d6b83708a28e26ad2aedbb3bf852ffc
greek_terms:
  - μόνην ὀρθὴν πολιτείαν
  - μεμιμημένας
  - εὐνόμους
  - ἄνευ νόμων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger concludes that the constitution meeting these terms is the only correct constitution, while the others are imitations, with lawful versions imitating toward finer things and the others toward more shameful things.
textual_basis: At 293e he says this constitution, according to such boundaries, must be called the only correct one. He then says the other constitutions are not genuine or really existing, but imitations of it, some called lawful in relation to finer things and the others in relation to more shameful things.
limits: This records the classification stated by the Stranger. Young Socrates' difficulty about ruling without laws is introduced here but not resolved within the marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0218
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 294a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 294a
  start_marker: 294a
  end_marker: 294a
  start_char: 75996
  end_char: 76446
  text_sha256: b7be194b99302fcc013f82611ecefdffe9b80d7e0affc66fd65239c4304874ae
greek_terms:
  - νομοθετική
  - βασιλικῆς
  - νόμους
  - φρονήσεως
  - βασιλικόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger grants that lawgiving somehow belongs to the royal art, but states that the best arrangement is not for laws to have authority, but for a royal man with practical intelligence to rule.
textual_basis: At 294a he notes that Young Socrates has objected especially to the correctness of ruling without laws. He then says that lawgiving is in some way part of the royal art, yet the best thing is not that laws have force but that the royal man with intelligence does.
limits: This records the subordination of lawgiving to royal expertise in the Stranger's argument. It does not yet give the reason for law's limitation, which follows in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0219
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 294b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 294b
  start_marker: 294b
  end_marker: 294b
  start_char: 76446
  end_char: 76832
  text_sha256: 1cbf664bf82249d4a59a521d3d5ea2582bd672b60660debc19e26cd94a2f103a
greek_terms:
  - δικαιότατον
  - βέλτιστον
  - ἀνομοιότητες
  - πράξεων
  - ἡσυχίαν
  - ἁπλοῦν
  - τέχνην
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger states why law cannot command the exact best and just rule for all cases: people and actions differ, and human affairs do not remain fixed."
textual_basis: The argument says exact universal command is blocked by differences among people and actions and by the instability of human affairs. It concludes that no art can declare one simple rule about all people for all time.
limits: This record does not include the personified-law simile in the following marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0220
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 294c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 294c
  start_marker: 294c
  end_marker: 294c
  start_char: 76832
  end_char: 77273
  text_sha256: f515d1cc094fc16669097d0c2b59e17c6e9cd47de7c9986c9d8421446cff9788
greek_terms:
  - αὐθάδη
  - ἀμαθῆ
  - τάξιν
  - ἁπλοῦν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger compares law to a stubborn and ignorant person who allows no action beyond his own order and no questioning, even when a new and better course occurs.
textual_basis: At 294c he says law tends toward this very thing, like a stubborn and ignorant human being who lets no one do anything beside his own order, nor ask questions, even if something new and better than his imposed account happens. He then concludes that a simple rule cannot fit matters that are never simple.
limits: The observation records the simile and its stated point about inflexibility. It does not claim that every legal order is useless or that the dialogue has abandoned lawgiving altogether.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0221
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 294d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 294d
  start_marker: 294d
  end_marker: 294d

  end_char: 77740
  text_sha256: 6cb806b550112401e7bcf4cfd09650318b9f587ad6622f7d366d677302ad6fec
greek_terms:
  - ἀσκήσεις
  - δρόμον
  - φιλονικίας
  - τέχνῃ γυμναζόντων
  - λεπτουργεῖν
  - σώματι
  - προστάττοντες
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger introduces group athletic training as the analogy for law: trainers working by art cannot prescribe a finely fitted regimen for each individual in mass competitive exercises."
textual_basis: The discussion turns from why law is not most exact to group exercises for contests. The Stranger recalls the commands of artful trainers and says they do not think individual fine-tuning is possible when prescribing what fits each body.
limits: This marker introduces the analogy but does not yet state the coarser group rule or the transfer to the lawgiver.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0222
source_work: Statesman

  - ἴσους πόνους
  - νομοθέτην
  - ἀγέλαις
  - δικαίου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The athletic analogy is completed as a model for general lawgiving: trainers set a coarser order for many bodies, and the lawgiver is then compared to one supervising herds concerning justice."
textual_basis: Trainers are said to make a coarser order for many bodies and to assign equal labors to groups that start and stop together. The marker then transfers the pattern to the lawgiver as supervisor of herds concerning justice.
limits: The marker only begins the move toward mutual dealings; the fuller account of contracts and written or unwritten law belongs to the following accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0223
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 295a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 295a
  start_marker: 295a
  end_marker: 295a
  start_char: 78150
  end_char: 78534
  text_sha256: 5319504752d8188a080849f418a4f7778bc7ef6a4280fbab307bc33d218c1bbc
greek_terms:
  - συμβολαίων
  - ἀκριβῶς
  - νόμον
  - γράμμασιν
  - ἀγραμμάτοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger says the lawgiver cannot, by ordering everyone together, give precisely what is fitting to each person, so law is set for the many and for the most part, whether in written form or in unwritten ancestral customs.
textual_basis: At 295a he says it is likely that the lawgiver supervising justice and contracts will never be able, by ordering all together, to distribute exactly what is fitting to each one. He therefore says the law will be laid down for the many, for the most part, and more coarsely, both in writings and in unwritten ancestral customs.
limits: This records the stated generality of lawmaking. It does not claim that written and unwritten laws are equivalent in every respect beyond this coarser rule-making function.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0224
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 295b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 295b
  start_marker: 295b
  end_marker: 295b
  start_char: 78534
  end_char: 78975
  text_sha256: 3a4720d2cc694d159becfdb44f61430e992b6eec3328d7e7ca53f7f23a3607c1
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὴν
  - ἐπιστήμην
  - δι’ ἀκριβείας
  - νόμους
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger argues that if someone truly possessed royal knowledge and could prescribe exactly what fits each person throughout life, that person would not set up written laws as obstacles for himself.
textual_basis: At 295b he asks how anyone could sit beside each person throughout life and always prescribe with precision what fits. He then says that anyone who truly had royal knowledge and could do this would hardly ever put obstacles in his own way by writing the laws just discussed.
limits: This records a hypothetical about exact royal expertise and written laws. It does not assert that such continuous supervision is actually present in any city.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0225
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 295c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 295c
  start_marker: 295c
  end_marker: 295c
  start_char: 78975
  end_char: 79395
  text_sha256: 9dc36fccceaeef271264d95fe425cd915f112e2a230321477bbec2d9cb4684b2
greek_terms:
  - ἰατρὸν
  - γυμναστικὸν
  - ὑπομνήματα
  - γράμματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger introduces a doctor or trainer who writes reminder notes before being absent, then asks whether, on returning sooner than expected, he would dare prescribe something different from the written notes when better conditions arise for the sick.
textual_basis: At 295c he proposes that a doctor or gymnastic trainer, expecting to be away from patients or trainees for a considerable time, would write reminders for them. He then asks whether, after returning in less time than expected, such an expert would not dare to recommend other things beyond those writings if different and better conditions occur for the sick.
limits: This observation records the medical-training analogy for revising written instructions. The marker does not complete the weather example, which follows in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0226
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 295d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 295d
  start_marker: 295d
  end_marker: 295d
  start_char: 79395
  end_char: 79775
  text_sha256: 6a83edb0a83710077e6c5c9909d297ff77ecf5ddc0c700abb1f8b9b01da407c6
greek_terms:
  - πνεύματα
  - νομοθετηθέντα
  - γραφέντα
  - ἰατρικὰ
  - ἔντεχνα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger develops the doctor example by treating rigid adherence to old written orders, despite unexpected winds or other changed conditions, as incompatible with knowledge and true art.
textual_basis: At 295d he asks whether the expert would think he must never depart from the old legislated instructions, neither prescribing other things himself nor allowing the patient to act beyond the writings, on the ground that only the written things are medical and healthy while other things are diseased and without art. He frames this as occurring within knowledge and true art.
limits: This records the negative example of treating written instructions as inflexible. It does not include the explicit conclusion about laughter, which appears at the start of the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0227
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 295e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 295e

  end_marker: 295e

  end_char: 80174
  text_sha256: f164bd68dc40021f0d1568183711ff849a7965a18308bf6841b30e8fefde30b6
greek_terms:
  - ἀληθεῖ τέχνῃ
  - νομοθετημάτων
  - δίκαια
  - ἄδικα
  - ἄγραφα
  - νομοθετήσαντι
  - ἀγέλαις
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger transfers the medical example back to lawgiving by asking whether written and unwritten civic laws should bind a later expert in the same rigid way.
textual_basis: The argument calls rigid expert adherence to such instructions laughable in true art, then turns to someone who has written or set down unwritten laws for human herds about just, unjust, noble, shameful, good, and bad things.
limits: The question about later expert prescription continues beyond this marker and leads into the common persuasion requirement in the next accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0228
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 296a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 296a
  start_marker: 296a
  end_marker: 296a

  end_char: 80585
  text_sha256: 6f121fb76c992af5512b52e5736496b6eb78a1ce6866ad3f082f77e3df0e8df0
greek_terms:
  - ἀπόρρημα
  - πολλῶν
  - βελτίους νόμους
  - πείσαντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger introduces the common saying that anyone who knows better laws than the old ones must legislate for his own city only after persuading each city, and not otherwise.
textual_basis: "At 296a he asks whether the prohibition against prescribing other things would appear laughable like the medical case. He then asks whether Young Socrates knows the argument said by the many on this matter, and reports it: if someone knows laws better than those before, he must legislate for his city after persuading it, otherwise not."
limits: This records a reported common argument, not the Stranger's final judgment of it. The answer to the common argument continues in the following markers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0229
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 296b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 296b
  start_marker: 296b
  end_marker: 296b
  start_char: 80585
  end_char: 81023
  text_sha256: 67ce0160b3cb404c1e9ac96ab03ae6cb32d7b38695638b418c700f8de83f39d3
greek_terms:
  - βιάζηται
  - ἰατρευόμενον
  - τέχνην
  - γεγραμμένα
  - βέλτιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger tests the persuasion requirement by returning to medicine: if a doctor with correct art forces a child, man, or woman to do what is better contrary to written instructions, the forced treatment should not be named a diseased error against the art."
textual_basis: "At 296b he asks what the name of force would be if someone forces the better without persuading. He then sets the medical case first: a doctor who has the art correctly compels a patient to do what is better contrary to the writings. He asks whether the name of this force would be anything rather than the diseased error said to be against the art."
limits: This records the medical test case. The political counterpart is introduced after the medical answer in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0230
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 296c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 296c
  start_marker: 296c
  end_marker: 296c
  start_char: 81023
  end_char: 81433
  text_sha256: bbdff155803d2f7f9d693d81c7f049de8c44dbc1de826b9c30a8bf39d17253f1
greek_terms:
  - νοσώδη
  - ἄτεχνα
  - πολιτικὴν τέχνην
  - αἰσχρὸν
  - ἄδικον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger applies the medical answer to politics by identifying the error against political art as what is shameful, bad, and unjust, then asks about people forced to do things more just, better, and finer than the written and ancestral rules.
textual_basis: At 296c Young Socrates agrees that the medically forced person can say anything except that he has suffered diseased and artless things from the doctors. The Stranger then asks what the error against political art is, and Young Socrates answers that it is the shameful, bad, and unjust. The Stranger next turns to those forced to act beside the written and ancestral rules in ways more just, better, and finer than the earlier ones.
limits: This records the criterion being set up for political force. The completed negative answer about blame comes in the following marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0231
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 296d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  - ἄδικα
  - κακὰ
  - πλούσιος
  - πένης
  - γράμματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger rejects a blame formula for beneficial political compulsion, then tests whether the wealth or poverty of the compeller can determine whether the compelled action is just.
textual_basis: The argument says criticism of such force would be laughable if it called the compelled people sufferers of shameful, unjust, and bad treatment. It then asks whether compulsion is just when performed by a rich person and unjust when performed by a poor person.
limits: The beneficial-action boundary is completed in the following marker; this record preserves only the rejection of blame terms and the wealth test.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0233

  - νόμον

  - πολιτεία
  - νόμων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The pilot analogy makes art rather than written rules the operative law: the pilot preserves fellow sailors by watching their advantage, and the same pattern is transferred to correct government."
textual_basis: The pilot is described as preserving fellow sailors by attending to their advantage and by providing art as law instead of setting down writings. The marker then applies the same pattern to capable rulers and a correct constitution in which the force of art is stronger than the laws.
limits: The condition under which intelligent rulers avoid error continues in the next accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0234
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 297b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  - νοῦ
  - τέχνης
  - δικαιότατον
  - σῴζειν
  - ἀμείνους
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger states the condition under which intelligent rulers do not err: they must continually distribute what is most just with intelligence and art, be able to preserve the people in the city, and make them better from worse as far as possible."
textual_basis: "At 297b he says there is no error for rulers with understanding as long as they guard one great thing: distributing the most just with intelligence and art, preserving those in the city, and making them better from worse within the possible."
limits: This records the stated correctness condition for expert rule. It does not prove that any actual rulers satisfy the condition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0235

greek_terms:
  - σμικρόν
  - ὀλίγον
  - ἕν
  - ὀρθήν
  - μιμήματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger locates the one correct constitution around a small number, the few, and the one, and classifies all the other constitutions as imitations of it, some imitating toward finer things and others toward worse things.
textual_basis: At 297c the claim about city management is completed by saying that the correct constitution must be sought around what is small, few, and one. The remaining constitutions are then set down as imitations, divided by whether they imitate this constitution toward finer or more shameful things.
limits: This records the classification stated in the exchange. Young Socrates' follow-up question shows that the account of imitation has not yet been explained.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0236
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 297d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 297d

  end_marker: 297d

  end_char: 83620
  text_sha256: 64f6d08c2692780549d1ed42b8b98a805c35a11a29f947a0a6cd65c3c52bb9f9
greek_terms:
  - ἁμάρτημα
  - ζητεῖν
  - ὀρθῆς
  - συγγράμμασι
  - σῴζεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger frames the next step as a difficult search for the present error in the argument. He introduces the question of how, if only the expert constitution is correct, other constitutions are preserved by using its written rules while doing the currently praised practice.
textual_basis: At 297d Young Socrates asks what the error is. The Stranger says that they must seek something not very usual or easy to see, and then asks whether, since only the stated constitution is correct, the others must be preserved by using its writings while doing what is now praised.
limits: This record marks the procedural setup for the next argument. The currently praised practice is not identified until the following marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0237
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 297e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 297e
  start_marker: 297e
  end_marker: 297e
  start_char: 83620
  end_char: 84203
  text_sha256: 9b040364bbb9b025f1e14e0461bb6369089970c7a534d8f0168bdd34130394b8
greek_terms:
  - νόμους
  - θανάτῳ
  - δεύτερον
  - εἰκόνας
  - κυβερνήτην
  - ἰατρόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger identifies the praised practice as forbidding anyone in the city to act contrary to the laws and punishing violators by death and the most extreme penalties. He calls this the best second arrangement once the first arrangement has been set aside, then returns to the images of the pilot and doctor to explain how it arises.
textual_basis: At 297e he says that no one should dare do anything contrary to the laws, and that whoever dares should be punished by death and the most extreme measures. He immediately calls this correct and finest as second, after the first thing just stated has been removed, and asks how this second arrangement came to be before returning to the pilot and doctor images.
limits: This records the Stranger's ranking of law-obedience as second within the argument. It does not endorse the penalties or claim that this second arrangement is the absolutely best political order.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0238
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 298a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 298a
  start_marker: 298a
  end_marker: 298a
  start_char: 84203
  end_char: 84599
  text_sha256: 99db2df44bef53da12523dc0234480c51dd6d1fa0c70fa8129e3e168bf10778b
greek_terms:
  - δεινότατα
  - σῴζειν
  - λωβᾶσθαι
  - τέμνοντες
  - κάοντες
  - φόρους
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger begins an imagined case in which people think they suffer terrible things from doctors and pilots. In the medical side of the case, the same experts who can save patients can also harm them through cutting, burning, and imposed expenses that are compared to taxes.
textual_basis: At 298a he asks them to imagine that everyone thought they suffered terribly from these experts. He says each expert saves whomever he wishes, but if he wishes to harm, he harms by cutting, burning, and ordering expenses to be brought to himself like taxes, spending little or none of them on the sick person and using the rest for himself and his servants.
limits: This is a constructed scenario inside the craft analogy. It does not assert that actual doctors and pilots behave this way.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0239
source_work: Statesman

  - κακουργοῦσιν
  - βουλευσαίμεθα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The abuse hypothetical escalates: doctors are imagined killing patients for payment, and pilots are imagined abandoning passengers, causing disasters at sea, throwing people into the water, and doing other wrongs."
textual_basis: The imagined doctors receive payment from relatives or enemies of a sick person and kill the patient. The marker then gives the pilot side of the hypothetical, including abandonment, disasters at sea, throwing people into the water, and other wrongdoing, before proposing deliberation about these crafts.
limits: This is a constructed accusation within the analogy, not a factual report about doctors or pilots.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - δημιουργῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger's imagined reform removes autonomous rule from medicine and navigation and transfers deliberation to an assembly, either of the whole people or only the wealthy. Private persons and other craftsmen are allowed to contribute opinions about voyages, diseases, drugs, medical tools, ships, and ship equipment.
textual_basis: At 298c the proposal is to no longer entrust either craft with autonomous rule over slaves or free people, but instead to gather an assembly of all or only the rich. The passage explicitly allows private persons and other craftsmen to offer opinions about sailing and illness, and about the use of medical drugs, medical instruments, ships, and nautical equipment.
limits: This records the imagined replacement of expert rule by assembly deliberation. It is part of an analogy, not a direct constitutional proposal.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἀνέμων
  - θαλάττης
  - λῃσταῖς
  - πλήθει
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The assembly procedure is extended to nautical hazards: winds, sea conditions, encounters with pirates, and possible naval battles. Whatever seems right to the multitude about these matters, whether advised by doctors, pilots, or private persons, is then to be written down."
textual_basis: At 298d the topics for public deliberation include the use of nautical tools, dangers connected with sailing, winds and sea, meetings with pirates, and naval battles with long ships. The text then says that what seems right to the multitude about these matters, whether on advice from doctors, pilots, or private persons, is to be written down.
limits: This records the procedure inside the imagined craft analogy. The placement of the written decisions in public records continues in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0242

greek_terms:
  - κύρβεσί
  - στήλαις
  - ἄγραφα
  - γράμματα
  - κληρούμενος
english_gloss: ""
observation: The imagined decisions are fixed on public records or as unwritten ancestral customs, and all later navigation and treatment of the sick must be conducted according to them. The hypothetical then adds yearly officials, chosen from the wealthy or from the whole people by lot, who command ships and treat patients according to the writings.
textual_basis: At 298e the decisions are written on public boards and pillars, while some are established as unwritten ancestral customs. The passage then says that all future sailing and treatments are to be performed according to these, and adds annual rulers from the multitude, selected either from the wealthy or from the whole people by lot, who act according to the writings.
limits: This records the hypothetical replacement of expert judgment by fixed writings and customs. It does not claim that medicine or navigation should in fact be run this way.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Former rulers are brought in for audit, and anyone who wishes may accuse them of not steering ships according to the writings or ancestral customs. The same procedure is applied to treatment of the sick, and conviction leads to an assessed penalty or payment.
limits: The court setup begins before this marker and is not restated here; this record preserves the audit and accusation action inside the current span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The hypothetical adds a law against investigating navigational or medical truth contrary to the writings: such an investigator is denied the craft names and is instead labeled as a speculative talker, sophist, and corrupter of younger people."
textual_basis: After Young Socrates accepts penalties for voluntary rule under the imagined system, the Stranger adds a law against someone investigating nautical or medical truth contrary to the writings and reasoning about such matters. The marker denies this person the names of doctor and pilot and applies hostile labels before beginning the corruption charge.
limits: The prosecution procedure, persuasion charge, and penalty continue in the following accepted observation.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0245

  - πείθειν
  - κολάζειν
  - σοφώτερον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The anti-inquiry law is completed by allowing prosecution of anyone who persuades young or old people to take up navigation or medicine contrary to the laws and written rules. If the accused is judged to be persuading in this way, the punishment is to be extreme, because nothing should be wiser than the laws.
textual_basis: At 299c the law permits any authorized person to bring an accuser into court against someone who urges autonomous command of ships or the sick contrary to the laws. The same marker states the penalty for persuading young or old people contrary to the laws and writings, and gives the reason that nothing should be wiser than the laws.
limits: This records the hypothetical legal machinery inside the craft analogy. It does not state that such prosecutions are actually just or that the dialogue endorses criminalizing inquiry.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - γεγραμμένα

  - θηρευτικῆς
  - γραφικῆς
  - μιμητικῆς
  - τεκτονικῆς
  - γεωργίας
  - συγγράμματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger generalizes the imagined written-rule regime beyond medicine and navigation by applying it to military art, hunting, painting, imitation, carpentry, manufacture, agriculture, plant care, horse-breeding, herd management, divination, and service arts.
textual_basis: The argument first refers back to written rules and ancestral customs, then asks what would happen if the same arrangement governed a list of other sciences and crafts, including military, hunting, painting, imitation, building, manufacture, agriculture, plant care, animal care, divination, and service arts.
limits: The list continues into the next marker, where games and arithmetic are added and the destruction of the arts is stated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0247
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  - πεττείαν
  - ἀριθμητικὴν
  - συγγράμματα
  - τέχνην
  - ἀπόλοιντο
  - ζητεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The generalized hypothetical ends by adding games and arithmetic to the list of practices governed by writings rather than art. Young Socrates answers that all the arts would be entirely destroyed and would not arise again, because this law prevents inquiry, making life altogether unlivable.
textual_basis: At 299e the list includes games and arithmetic in several forms, and the Stranger frames the question as practices being done according to writings and not according to art. Young Socrates replies that all the arts would perish and not come back because the law blocks inquiry.
limits: This records the consequence stated within the hypothetical. It does not claim that written rules always destroy a craft outside this constructed case.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0248
source_work: Statesman

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  text_sha256: 14b11f1afba6a2edc7de1cb93ff992fcf4d34db9ee2fe449c03f104630dc07ae
greek_terms:
  - συγγράμματα
  - ἐπιστατεῖν
  - χειροτονηθέντα
  - λαχόντα
  - κέρδους
  - χάριτος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger then tests the opposite danger within the written-rule regime: an elected or randomly selected supervisor might ignore the writings and act otherwise for profit or private favor while knowing nothing. He asks whether that would be an even greater evil than the earlier case."
textual_basis: At 300a the practices already named are imagined as forced to occur according to writings, with an elected or lot-chosen person supervising the writings. The case adds that this supervisor neglects the writings and acts contrary to them for gain or private favor while lacking knowledge, and asks whether this is a greater evil.
limits: This records the danger used to defend the second-best rule of obedience to writings. It does not claim that elected or lot-selected offices are always corrupt.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0249

  - συμβούλων
  - πλῆθος
  - ἁμαρτήματος
  - συγγραμμάτων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The reason for treating ignorant departure from the writings as worse is that the laws have been set from much experience, with advisers persuading the multitude to establish each rule. Someone who dares to act contrary to them multiplies error and overturns every practice more severely than the writings do.
textual_basis: At 300b the laws are described as laid down from much experience and from advisers who have counselled well and persuaded the multitude to set each rule. The person acting contrary to these rules is said to make an error many times over and to overturn all practice more than the writings.
limits: This records the rationale for rule-obedience under the second-best arrangement. It does not make written law the best rule in the surrounding argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0250

  - δεύτερος πλοῦς
  - μιμήματα
  - ἀληθείας
  - εἰδότων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger states the second-best rule for those who establish laws and writings: neither one person nor a multitude should ever be allowed to act contrary to them. He then classifies such written rules as imitations of the truth, written as far as possible by those who know."
textual_basis: At 300c the second voyage is named as not allowing either one or many to do anything at all contrary to laws and writings. The same marker then says these rules are imitations of the truth, insofar as they have been written by knowledgeable people.
limits: This records the second-best rule and its imitation status. The contrast with what the true knowledgeable politician does continues into the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0251
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 300d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  end_char: 90171

greek_terms:
  - βελτίω
  - γεγραμμένα
  - νόμοι
  - πλῆθος
  - ἀληθινὸς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger resumes the true-politician contrast: the knowledgeable ruler may find something better than his own written instructions sent to absent people. He then says that any individual or multitude that tries to do something contrary to established laws as something better is, as far as it can, doing the same thing as the true ruler."
textual_basis: At 300d the better alternative is explicitly set against what the ruler had himself written and sent to absent people. The question then extends the same pattern to any one person or any multitude with established laws who attempts to act contrary to those laws on the ground that something else is better.
limits: This records the formal similarity between expert and non-expert departures from written rules. The next marker supplies the criterion separating bad imitation from the true case.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0252
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 300e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 300e
  start_marker: 300e
  end_marker: 300e
  start_char: 90171
  end_char: 90634
  text_sha256: e36b666755641490ea36ff27c28bceed69b74244d151addadb2d70c2ee28dcd5
greek_terms:
  - ἔντεχνοι
  - ἀληθέστατον
  - πλῆθος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger separates two cases of acting beyond written rules: if non-knowers do it, they attempt to imitate the truth but imitate it very badly; if expert practitioners do it, the act is no longer imitation but the truest thing itself. He then recalls the prior agreement that no multitude can acquire any art."
textual_basis: At 300e the distinction is made by whether the actors lack knowledge or possess art. Non-knowers are said to imitate the truth badly, while experts are said to enact the truest thing rather than an imitation; the marker ends by recalling that no multitude can take hold of an art.
limits: This records the classification criterion stated in the passage. It does not identify which actual cities, if any, contain the expert case.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0253
source_work: Statesman

  - τέχνης
  - πολιτείαν
  - νόμων
  - ἀριστοκρατίαν
  - ὀλιγαρχίαν
english_gloss: true constitution, art, laws, aristocracy, oligarchy
observation: The Stranger classifies rule by the wealthy according to its relation to written laws and ancestral customs. When wealthy rulers imitate the true expert constitution by not acting against the writings and customs, the form is called aristocracy; when they disregard the laws, it is called oligarchy.
textual_basis: At 301a the lawful imitation is described as doing nothing against written rules and ancestral customs. The Stranger then says that when the wealthy imitate this arrangement the constitution is called aristocracy, but when they do not care for the laws it is called oligarchy.
limits: This records the naming contrast inside the constitutional classification. It does not evaluate whether wealthy rule actually possesses expertise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0254
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 301b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 301b
  start_marker: 301b
  end_marker: 301b
  start_char: 91065
  end_char: 91467
  text_sha256: 78887f9f812f023f7ca7ecc7133845b344b78dba9de35ec842725a5ee4a107b3
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμονα
  - βασιλέα
  - ἐπιστήμης
  - δόξης
  - μοναρχοῦντα
  - πολιτειῶν
english_gloss: knower, king, knowledge, opinion, monarchy, constitutions
observation: The Stranger says that a single ruler who rules according to laws while imitating the knower is called king, without a separate name for rule based on knowledge and rule based on opinion under law. He adds that even a truly knowledgeable single ruler would receive the same name, reducing the current five constitutional names to one in that case.
textual_basis: At 301b the name king is applied to the law-abiding single ruler who imitates the knowledgeable person, and the text says the name does not distinguish rule with knowledge from rule with opinion under laws. The marker then says that a genuinely knowledgeable single ruler would still be called king, so that one of the five current constitutional names remains.
limits: This records the naming rule for the single ruler. The marker only begins the question about a single ruler acting apart from laws and customs.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0255
source_work: Statesman

  - ἄγνοια
  - μιμήματος
  - τύραννον
english_gloss: writings, best, desire, ignorance, imitation, tyrant
observation: The Stranger names tyranny as the case in which a single ruler acts neither according to laws nor according to customs, while claiming like the knower that the best action must sometimes go beyond the writings, but with desire and ignorance leading the imitation.
textual_basis: At 301c the single ruler is described as pretending, like the knowledgeable ruler, that what is best should be done contrary to the writings. The marker makes desire and ignorance the guides of this imitation and says such a person should be called tyrant.
limits: This records the naming criterion stated for tyranny. It does not assess the broader claim about why the five constitutional names arose, which continues beyond this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0256

  - διακυβερνῶντα
  - ὀρθὴν
  - πολιτείαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger contrasts distrust of one-person rule with the correct case: people fear discretionary harm, but a ruler who rules with virtue and knowledge and distributes just and holy things correctly would govern the only exactly correct constitution."
textual_basis: The feared one-person ruler is described as able to harm, kill, and injure at will. The same marker sets this against a ruler acting with virtue and knowledge, distributing just and holy things correctly to all, and happily governing the only exactly correct constitution.
limits: This record does not include the following explanation for why cities turn to written documents in the absence of such a ruler.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0257
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 301e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 301e

  end_marker: 301e

greek_terms:
  - βασιλεὺς
  - σμήνεσιν
  - συγγράμματα
  - ἀληθεστάτης πολιτείας
  - ἴχνη
  - γράμματα
english_gloss: king, swarms, writings, truest constitution, traces, letters
observation: The Stranger explains the turn to written rules by saying that no king of the described kind naturally arises in cities as one does in swarms, already differing in body and soul. In that absence, people must gather together to write documents while running after the traces of the truest constitution.
textual_basis: At 301e the absence of such a king in cities is paired with the practical need to come together and write documents. The activity is described as pursuing traces of the truest constitution, and the marker begins to describe constitutions founded on letters and customs without knowledge.
limits: This records the stated reason for written rules in the second-best setting. The effects of that foundation are completed in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0258
source_work: Statesman

  - πλοῖα
  - κυβερνητῶν
  - ναυτῶν
  - μοχθηρίαν
  - ἄγνοιαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger compares many cities to ships that sink because of bad pilots and sailors who are deeply ignorant about the greatest matters, while also noting the natural strength by which some cities remain stable for a long time.
textual_basis: The city is described as naturally strong because some cities remain stable and are not overturned for a very long time. The same marker compares many cities to ships that sink and are destroyed because of the badness and ignorance of pilots and sailors.
limits: This records only the city-ship comparison and its stated cause; the following marker completes the charge that political actors think they possess knowledge they lack.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0259
source_work: Statesman

  - ἥκιστα χαλεπὴ
  - βαρυτάτη
  - πάρεργον
english_gloss: political matters, sciences, incorrect constitutions, least harsh, heaviest, side matter
observation: "The Stranger completes the charge that those who know nothing about political matters think they possess this science most clearly, then introduces a new evaluative task: among the incorrect constitutions, they should inspect which is least harsh to live with and which is heaviest, even though this is called a side matter relative to the present proposal."
textual_basis: At 302b the people concerned with political matters are said to know nothing about them while thinking they possess this science most clearly. The Stranger then asks which of the incorrect constitutions is least harsh to live with and which is most burdensome, marking the inquiry as a side matter for the current topic.
limits: This records the procedural turn to ranking incorrect constitutions. The answer to that ranking is not yet supplied within this marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἑβδόμην
english_gloss: monarchy, rule of few, many, cutting in two, six, seventh
observation: The Stranger restates the three constitutional bases as monarchy, rule by the few, and rule by the many, then proposes dividing each of the three in two to make six, while separating the correct constitution from them as a seventh.
textual_basis: At 302c the Stranger says the same one among the three can become both especially harsh and easiest. He then names monarchy, rule of the few, and rule of the many as the three forms from the beginning of the present discussion, and proposes cutting each in two to produce six while setting the correct one apart as a seventh.
limits: This records the proposed division of the constitutional forms. It does not supply the later ranking of the six forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0261
source_work: Statesman

  end_marker: 302d

  end_char: 94369
  text_sha256: 5bed33585afc06c0680c6bed3a271acf5950fd1c34a09abf3c5db2cd03fd1473
greek_terms:
  - μοναρχίας
  - τυραννικήν
  - ἀριστοκρατίαν
  - ὀλιγαρχίαν
  - δημοκρατίαν
  - διπλῆν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger reviews the paired names already derived from one-person and few-person rule, then says democracy, previously treated as simple, must now also be treated as double.
textual_basis: At 302d, the Stranger says monarchy yielded kingship and tyranny, rule of the not-many yielded aristocracy and oligarchy, and rule of the many had earlier been set down simply under the name democracy. He now says democracy too must be posited as double, prompting Young Socrates to ask how it is to be divided.
limits: This records the reopening of democracy as a twofold regime type. It does not yet state the dividing criterion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - παράνομον

  - διχοτομεῖ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Lawful and unlawful rule become the divider for democracy and for the other non-true constitutions after the correct constitution has been set aside.
textual_basis: The Stranger says that ruling according to laws and unlawfully belongs to democracy and the other regimes. He explains that this cut was not useful while they were seeking the correct constitution, but after that constitution is removed, lawful and unlawful rule divide each of the remaining constitutions.
limits: This record covers the legality divider only; the monarchy ranking in the same marker is separated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0304
source_work: Statesman

greek_terms:
  - μοναρχία
  - γράμμασιν
  - ἀγαθοῖς
  - νόμους
  - ἀρίστη
  - ἄνομος
  - χαλεπὴ
  - βαρυτάτη
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Monarchy is ranked by its relation to good written laws: monarchy joined to such laws is best among the six non-true constitutions, while lawless monarchy is the harshest and most burdensome to live with."
textual_basis: After the legality divider has been stated, the Stranger says monarchy yoked to good writings called laws is best of all six constitutions, while lawless monarchy is harsh and most burdensome to share life with.
limits: This record concerns only the monarchy ranking. The ranking of few-rule and democracy is handled nearby.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0263
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 303a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 303a
  start_marker: 303a
  end_marker: 303a
  start_char: 94907
  end_char: 95314
  text_sha256: 7ba54af9ae6dcb00c99bb06cda4578af3dff9c551be9f88bd6d177c4238d2a2d
greek_terms:
  - μέσην
  - πλήθους
  - ἀσθενῆ
  - ἀρχὰς
  - νομίμων
  - χειρίστη
  - παρανόμων
  - βελτίστη
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The rule of the few is ranked in the middle between one-person and many-person rule, while democracy is described as weak because offices are distributed in small shares among many. This weakness reverses its evaluation: among lawful constitutions democracy is worst, but among unlawful constitutions it is best."
textual_basis: At 303a, the Stranger says the rule of the not-many should be regarded as middle in both directions, just as the few are between one and multitude. He then says the multitude's regime is weak in every respect and unable to do any great good or evil compared with the others because offices are distributed in small portions among many; for that reason it is worst when the constitutions are lawful and best when all are unlawful.
limits: This records the ranking logic tied to democracy's distributed weakness. It does not yet include the final separation of the seventh constitution.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0264
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 303b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 303b
  start_marker: 303b
  end_marker: 303b
  start_char: 95314
  end_char: 95694
  text_sha256: b25319300a8fee810d356c70e2ae4d01774d54d9c356f07a22b61e066c3a9503
greek_terms:
  - ἀκολάστων
  - δημοκρατίᾳ
  - κοσμίων
  - πρώτῃ
  - ἑβδόμης
  - θεὸν
  - πολιτειῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The ranking is summarized by condition: if all regimes are undisciplined, living in democracy wins; if all are orderly, democracy is least livable, while the first regime is best except for the seventh. The seventh constitution must be separated from all the others like a god from human beings."
textual_basis: At 303b, the Stranger says that among undisciplined regimes living in democracy prevails, while among orderly regimes one should least live in democracy. He adds that in the first regime one lives by far first and best, except for the seventh, because that one must be distinguished from all the other constitutions like a god from human beings. Young Socrates agrees that these things seem to turn out this way and should be handled as stated.
limits: This records the summary ranking and separation of the seventh constitution. It does not identify any actual historical constitution with the seventh.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0265

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  end_char: 96123
  text_sha256: b880da7f611e3192a8f59fcecb2d15fa0dd747f6323d9a2aa2bb85ae239524ff
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμονος
  - στασιαστικούς
  - εἰδώλων
  - γόητας
  - σοφιστῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After separating the knowledgeable constitution, the Stranger says participants in all the other constitutions should be removed from the name political: they are not politicians but factional actors, leaders of the greatest images, themselves image-like, the greatest imitators and enchanters, and sophists of sophists."
textual_basis: At 303c, the Stranger says those sharing in all constitutions except the knowledgeable one must be stripped away as not political but factional. He describes them as presiding over the greatest images, being such images themselves, and becoming the greatest imitators and enchanters among sophists; Young Socrates agrees that the phrase has been turned back correctly onto those called political.
limits: This records the classification of non-knowledgeable political actors. It does not identify particular historical speakers or officeholders.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0266
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 303d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  end_char: 96545

  - τέχνης
  - συγγενές
  - βασιλικῷ
  - χρυσὸν
  - καθαίρουσι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After separating the false political troupe with difficulty, the Stranger marks a harder remaining task: another class is closer to the royal kind and harder to understand, so the inquiry is compared to gold purification."
textual_basis: The previously separated group is described as a troupe that had to be divided from political art and has only just been separated with great difficulty. The Stranger then says a harder case remains because it is more related to the royal kind and harder to learn, and he introduces the gold-purification comparison.
limits: The material details of the refining comparison belong to the following record, and the application to auxiliary arts belongs to the accepted record after that.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0267

  - βασάνων

english_gloss: ""
observation: "The refining comparison is spelled out: after alien materials are removed, valuable things related to gold remain mixed with it and are separated only through fire and testing until pure gold can be seen by itself."
textual_basis: The Stranger says precious materials related to gold remain mixed in after the first separation and names metals and testing by fire as the way they are removed, allowing pure gold to be seen alone.
limits: The end of the marker begins applying the analogy to related arts, but that application is already covered by the following accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0268
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 303e-304a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt
  stephanus_span: 303e-304a
  start_marker: 303e
  end_marker: 304a
  start_char: 96545
  end_char: 97435
  text_sha256: 22ca1e984545972eba3cc3906ef9cce14bc11d915fa1dcb5a745c9f4c9fb4062
greek_terms:
  - πολιτικῆς
  - ἐπιστήμης
  - στρατηγία
  - δικαστικὴ
  - ῥητορεία
  - βασιλικῇ
  - συνδιακυβερνᾷ
  - μουσικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The refining analogy is applied to political science: alien and unfriendly things have been separated, but valuable and related arts remain, including generalship, judging, and rhetoric that shares with kingship by persuading about justice and co-governing civic actions. The Stranger asks how to separate these so the sought object appears naked and alone, then proposes to make it clear through music."
textual_basis: At 303e-304a, the Stranger says alien and unfriendly things have been separated from political knowledge, while precious related things remain. He names generalship, judging, and rhetoric insofar as it shares with kingship, persuades what is just, and co-governs actions in cities. He then asks how someone might divide these off and show the searched-for thing bare and alone by itself, before saying that for the sake of trial it should be made clear through music.
limits: This records the list of related arts and the new separation problem. It does not yet state the musical analogy's content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - χειροτεχνίας
  - ἐπιστημῶν
  - μανθάνειν
  - ἄρχειν
english_gloss: music, learning, handcraft, sciences, learning, ruling
observation: The Stranger separates first-order learning in music and handcraft sciences from a further science that decides whether those subjects should be learned, and Young Socrates agrees that the further science is different.
textual_basis: At 304b, music and handcraft sciences are named as learnable disciplines, then the Stranger asks whether the inquiry into whether to learn any of them is itself another science concerning those same matters. Young Socrates affirms that it is and agrees that it is different from the earlier sciences.
limits: This records the distinction between learning a craft and deciding whether to learn it. The marker raises the rule-ranking question but does not answer it inside this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0270

  - πείθειν
  - πειστικὸν
  - ἐπιστήμῃ
english_gloss: overseeing, ruling, learning, teaching, persuading, persuasive, science
observation: "The deciding science is made supervisory: the science deciding whether to learn rules the learned and teaching science, and the science deciding whether to persuade rules the persuasive capacity."
textual_basis: At 304c, Young Socrates says the deciding science should rule all the other sciences. The Stranger restates this for the science that decides whether to learn over the learned and teaching science, and likewise for the science that decides whether to persuade over the capacity to persuade.
limits: This records the supervisory pattern for learning and persuasion. The marker opens the question of assigning mass persuasion through storytelling, but it does not give that assignment inside this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0271
```
