```yaml
  - πειθοῦς
  - βίας
  - πολιτικοῦ
english_gloss: multitude, crowd, storytelling, teaching, rhetoric, persuasion, force, political
observation: Persuasion of a crowd through storytelling rather than instruction is assigned to rhetoric, while the decision whether to act by persuasion, force, or inaction is assigned to the political power as ruler of persuasive and speech capacities.
textual_basis: At 304d, Young Socrates says it is plain that the crowd-persuasion capacity belongs to rhetoric. The Stranger then asks which science decides whether to use persuasion, force, or rest, and Young Socrates assigns it to the science ruling persuasive and speech powers; the Stranger identifies that power as political.
limits: This records the hierarchy between political decision and rhetoric or speech within this marker. It does not give a full taxonomy of rhetoric.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - πολεμικὴ
  - φιλίας
  - διαβουλεύσασθαι
  - ἑτέραν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Military practice is treated as artful execution, while the knowledge that decides whether to make war or settle through friendship is separated from that military craft.
textual_basis: Young Socrates grants that the power by which generalship and military practice act is artful. The Stranger then asks whether the knowledge that deliberates about war or friendship is the same as or different from that craft, and Young Socrates answers that it must be different.
limits: The opening sentence repeats the rhetoric separation already handled by the previous accepted observation, and the royal mastery of military craft is stated in the next accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0273

  - ὑπηρετικήν
  - στρατηγῶν
english_gloss: ruling, art, military, mistress, royal, political, subordinate, generals
observation: The deliberative power over war is said to rule military craft, and the truly royal craft is named as the only master of the whole war craft; generals' knowledge is denied political status and called subordinate.
textual_basis: At 305a, the Stranger asks whether the war-deliberating science should rule the military science, and Young Socrates agrees. He then asks what could be master of the whole military art except the truly royal craft, and Young Socrates says none other. The generals' science is then said not to be political because it is subordinate.
limits: This records the hierarchy of royal, military, and generals' expertise. It does not classify every form of military practice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The correct judge's power is framed as receiving legal rules from a lawgiver-king and judging matters by looking to those rules while resisting bribes, fears, and pity.
textual_basis: At 305b, the Stranger asks whether the power of correct judges extends beyond taking all legal norms for contracts from a lawgiver-king and using them to determine just and unjust matters. The marker also names the judge's own virtue as not being overcome by gifts, fears, or pity.
limits: This record stays with the legal application and impartiality stated inside the marker. It does not by itself state the final classification of judicial power.
```

```yaml

  - φύλακα
  - ὑπηρέτιν
  - πολιτική
english_gloss: enmity, friendship, lawgiver, judges, royal, laws, guardian, servant, political
observation: Judicial power is described as not royal but as guardian of laws and servant of the royal power; after this, the Stranger observes that none of the sciences reviewed has appeared political.
textual_basis: At 305c, the judicial case includes resistance to enmity and friendship against the lawgiver's order. Young Socrates confines the judicial function to the work just described, and the Stranger says judges' strength is not royal but a guard of laws and servant of that power. He then says, considering all the sciences spoken of together, none has appeared political.
limits: This records the negative classification and service role of judges. It does not identify the political science positively.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0276
source_work: Statesman

  text_sha256: 0c1ff9b5390e89c58fa04bb727989fafc2bdb6470c2ff4d33ee628e70a7d85b4
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὴν
  - ἄρχειν
  - πράττειν
  - γιγνώσκουσαν
  - ἐγκαιρίας
  - ἀκαιρίας
  - προσταχθέντα
  - ἰδίαν
english_gloss: royal, ruling, acting, knowing, timeliness, untimeliness, ordered things, own
observation: The truly royal craft is said not to perform the actions itself, but to rule those who can perform them by knowing the beginnings and impulses of the greatest civic actions with respect to good and bad timing; the other sciences do the ordered tasks and keep names from their own actions.
textual_basis: At 305d, the Stranger states that royal knowledge should rule practitioners rather than act itself, because it knows the starting point and impulse of major city actions concerning timely and untimely moments. He adds that the other sciences just passed through neither rule one another nor themselves, each being concerned with its own action and rightly receiving its own name from that action.
limits: This records the summary hierarchy of ruling knowledge and executing sciences. It does not specify any concrete civic action or identify a historical ruler.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0277
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 305e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  - πολιτικήν
  - συνυφαίνουσαν
  - ὑφαντικῆς παράδειγμα
english_gloss: political art, weaving together, weaving paradigm
observation: The Stranger names the power that rules laws and cares for all city matters political, then proposes to examine it through the already established weaving paradigm now that the city kinds have become visible.
textual_basis: At 305e, the Stranger gathers the ruling and caring power under the common name political and describes it as weaving everything together. He then asks whether they should now go through it according to the paradigm of weaving, and Young Socrates strongly agrees.
limits: This records the explicit transfer of the weaving paradigm back to the political target. The marker does not yet state the content of the political fabric.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0278

  - ὕφασμα
  - ἀρετῆς μέρος
english_gloss: royal interweaving, fabric, part of virtue
observation: The Stranger frames the next task as identifying the royal interweaving and the fabric it produces, then introduces a difficult claim that one part of virtue differs in kind from another.
textual_basis: At 306a, the Stranger says they must state what the royal interweaving is, how it interweaves, and what fabric it provides. He immediately marks the next claim as difficult and says that a part of virtue is in some way different in kind from another part of virtue.
limits: The marker introduces the virtue-part difference but breaks off before the named parts are fully stated. The record therefore stays with the setup and does not identify the complete pair from later markers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0279
source_work: Statesman

  start_char: 101719
  end_char: 102127
  text_sha256: c03f1e681607591382518f9c83bf0e1e6c07173c93c7cbed7c3daded1b0a56a2
greek_terms:
  - σωφροσύνην
  - ἀρετῆς
  - ἔχθραν
  - στάσιν
english_gloss: courage, moderation, virtue, enmity, faction
observation: Courage and moderation are first counted as distinct parts of virtue, and the Stranger then proposes the unusual claim that they stand in enmity and opposing faction in many things.
textual_basis: "At 306b, the Stranger secures agreement that courage is one part of virtue and that moderation is another part of the same whole. He then says they must dare a strange account about these parts: in some way they have enmity and opposing faction toward one another in many beings."
limits: This records the oppositional claim as proposed in the dialogue. It does not decide whether the claim is true or how the later examples will support it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0280

  end_char: 102547
  text_sha256: 9c3e72e9f5c9933395264f2edb8c04c0788fb4459001e591b9774e0e8dbe13a5
greek_terms:
  - ἀρετῆς μόρια
  - φίλια
  - διαφορὰν
  - καλὰ
  - ἐναντία
english_gloss: parts of virtue, friendly, difference, fine things, opposites
observation: "The Stranger turns the ordinary claim that virtue parts are friendly into a test: they should look among things called fine and ask whether these are set into two opposed forms."
textual_basis: At 306c, the Stranger acknowledges that the parts of virtue are usually said to be friendly to one another. He then asks whether this is simple or whether there is difference among kindred items, and directs the inquiry to all cases where things are called fine but placed into two forms opposed to each other.
limits: This records the method of testing the virtue-parts claim through paired opposites. The concrete examples only begin at the end of the marker and are not exhausted here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ἀνδρικόν

observation: "The Stranger identifies a shared praise-name for speed, intensity, and sharpness across thought, body, and voice: these different cases are praised by applying the name of courage or manliness to them."
textual_basis: At 306e, the Stranger says that when people admire speed, intensity, and sharpness in many actions, including thought, body, and voice, they praise them with one address connected to courage. He then lists sharp, fast, and intense cases as receiving the same common name.
limits: This records the naming pattern for one evaluative side of the opposition. It does not claim that every use of courage in the dialogue is merely a praise-name.

```

```yaml
english_gloss: calm, quiet, moderate, slow, soft, smooth, deep
observation: "The Stranger gives the opposed praise-pattern: quietness, slowness, softness, smoothness, and depth are admired across thought, action, voice, rhythm, and music under names connected with moderation and orderliness."
textual_basis: At 307a-307b, the Stranger asks whether the calm kind has often been praised in many actions and marks it as opposite to the preceding side. He then lists quiet and moderate cases in thought, slow and soft cases in actions, and smooth and deep cases in voices before extending the pattern to rhythm and music, where slowness used at the due moment is named by orderliness rather than courage.
limits: This record follows the opposed praise-pattern through the orderliness name. It does not include the blame-name reversal that begins later in 307b.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - μαλακώτερα
  - δειλὰ
  - βλακικά
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger makes timing govern evaluative names: fitting slowness receives an orderly name, but when either opposed tendency is untimely, speakers shift to blame-names for the sharp and fast side and for the heavy and slow side."
textual_basis: Fitting slowness in music receives the orderly name rather than the courageous name. The Stranger then says that when both opposed tendencies become untimely, the names are reassigned for blame, first for sharper, faster, harder cases and then for heavier, slower, softer cases.
limits: This record stops with the paired blame-name reversal. The later account of opposed character types becoming civic faction is handled by the following accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0285
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 307c-307d

  text_sha256: aa02ca00f6b1ba7f25499eac765731c3dcaf795ba8b946fae81618615700b6e9
greek_terms:
  - σώφρονα φύσιν
  - ἀνδρείαν
  - πολεμίαν
  - στάσιν
  - μειγνυμένας
  - συγγένειαν
  - ἔχθραν
  - πόλεσιν
english_gloss: moderate nature, courage, hostile, faction, mixing, kinship, enmity, cities
observation: The Stranger presents moderation and courage as opposed character forms that usually do not mix in actions or souls; each type praises what is kindred to itself and blames what differs, producing mutual enmity that becomes a civic disease in the greatest matters.
textual_basis: At 307c-307d, heavier, slower, softer things are associated with cowardly or sluggish traits, while the moderate nature and courage are described as opposite forms that stand like hostile factions and are not found mixing with each other in relevant actions. The Stranger then says people possessing these forms in their souls differ from each other, praise what is their own as kindred, blame different things as alien, and come into much enmity; he calls this difference playful in smaller cases but the most hateful disease for cities in the greatest matters.
limits: This records the opposition and factional effect of the two character forms. It does not yet state the political craft's remedy for mixing them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0286
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 307e-308a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  start_marker: 307e
  end_marker: 308a
  start_char: 105163
  end_char: 106121
  text_sha256: ddf6e3917f66c16c623146c2872c96e2bcefe966846047b2e10f824f157ccbf1
greek_terms:
  - κόσμιοι
  - ἥσυχον
  - εἰρήνην
  - ἔρωτα
  - ἀκαιρότερον
  - ἀπολέμως
  - ἐλευθέρων
  - δοῦλοι
english_gloss: orderly, quiet, peace, desire, more untimely, unwarlike, free, slaves
observation: "The Stranger diagnoses one civic danger of an excessive orderly disposition: people who always choose a quiet life and pursue peace beyond the proper time make themselves and the young unwarlike, remain exposed to attackers, and can become enslaved instead of free."
textual_basis: At 307e-308a, the orderly type is described as ready to live a quiet life, mind its own affairs, maintain similar relations at home, and preserve peace toward outside cities. Because this desire is more untimely than it should be, when such people get their way they become unwarlike, educate the young likewise, are always subject to attackers, and in a few years can turn themselves, their children, and the whole city from free people into slaves.
limits: This records the stated danger of excessive quietness and peace-seeking. It does not make a general anti-peace claim, since the passage specifies excess and bad timing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0287
source_work: Statesman

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  end_char: 106121
  text_sha256: 08f9dd2c57e804cdc04fbfa5f2b882ae83409fc318f5123290ea84409a1c28e5
greek_terms:
  - ἀνδρείαν
  - πόλεμον
  - συντείνοντες
  - σφοδροτέραν
  - ἐπιθυμίαν
  - ἔχθραν
  - διώλεσαν
  - δούλας
english_gloss: courage, war, stretching toward, more vehement, desire, enmity, destroyed, enslaved
observation: "The opposite civic danger is attached to excessive courage: cities bent continually toward war through an over-strong desire for that way of life make powerful enemies and either destroy their fatherlands outright or subject them to enemies."
textual_basis: At 308a, after Young Socrates calls the prior effect harsh and terrible, the Stranger asks about those leaning more toward courage. He describes them as always tightening their cities toward some war because their desire for such a life is stronger than it should be; by entering enmity with many powerful opponents, they either destroy their cities entirely or make their fatherlands enslaved and subject to enemies.
limits: This records the stated danger of excessive war-eagerness. It does not deny the positive role of courage elsewhere, since the problem named here is excess beyond what is needed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - διαφέρεσθον φύσει
  - τόδε τοίνυν αὖ λάβωμεν
english_gloss: enmity, faction, parts of virtue, differ by nature, let us take this next
observation: The Stranger closes the preceding examples by saying that the inquiry has found what it was looking for at the start, namely that substantial parts of virtue differ by nature and make their possessors do the same, then opens a new item for consideration.
textual_basis: At 308b, the Stranger asks why they should not say that the two kinds have the greatest enmity and faction toward one another. He then says they have found what was being examined at the beginning, that not-small parts of virtue differ by nature and make those who possess them do likewise, before saying they should take up the next point.
limits: This records a procedural recapitulation and transition. It does not restate all evidence for the virtue-part opposition from the preceding markers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - εἰς ἓν
  - δύναμιν
  - ἰδέαν
english_gloss: composite sciences, bad, good, rejects, suitable, into one, power, form
observation: "The Stranger states a rule for composite sciences: such a science does not voluntarily compose even a trivial work from bad and good materials, but rejects bad items where possible and gathers suitable good items, whether similar or dissimilar, into one power and form."
textual_basis: At 308c, the question contrasts a composite science willingly putting together its work from bad and good items with the alternative that every science everywhere rejects bad things as far as possible, takes suitable and good things, and brings similar and dissimilar items into one to create a single power and form. Young Socrates assents.
limits: This records the craft rule stated in the analogy. It does not yet apply the rule to political composition, which begins in the next marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0290
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 308d-308e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

greek_terms:
  - πολιτικὴ
  - χρηστῶν καὶ κακῶν ἀνθρώπων
  - βασανιεῖ
  - παιδεύειν
  - ὑπηρετεῖν
  - προστάττουσα
  - ἐπιστατοῦσα
  - ὑφαντικὴ
  - συμπλοκήν
english_gloss: political craft, good and bad people, will test, educate, serve, ordering, supervising, weaving, interweaving
observation: "The Stranger applies the composite-craft rule to politics: the true political craft will not willingly compose a city from good and bad people, but first tests them and then hands them to educators and assistants while itself orders and supervises the preparatory work, as weaving does with its preparers."
textual_basis: At 308d-308e, the political craft is said not to compose a city from good and bad human beings, but first to test in play and then deliver the tested material to those able to educate and assist. The Stranger compares this to weaving, which commands and oversees those who card and prepare other things for its interweaving, showing each subordinate the work it judges suitable.
limits: This records the master-craft hierarchy and testing step. It does not yet specify the later sorting of human types into punishment, servile placement, or woven strands.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_statesman_0291
source_work: Statesman
stephanus_span: 308e-309a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/statesman.txt

  end_char: 108218
  text_sha256: 361fa589e8fad45f28ceb203107b02efcfd50b73353139c53c6c405fb54f6cc9
greek_terms:
  - βασιλικὴ
  - παιδευταῖς
  - τροφεῦσιν
  - σύγκρασιν
  - ἦθός
  - ἀνδρείου καὶ σώφρονος
  - ἀθεότητα
  - ὕβριν
  - ἀδικίαν
  - θανάτοις
  - φυγαῖς
english_gloss: royal craft, educators, nurturers, mixture, character, courageous and moderate, godlessness, insolence, injustice, deaths, exiles
observation: The royal craft is described as directing lawful educators and nurturers so that they train only characters fitting its own mixture, while those unable to share courageous, moderate, and other virtue-oriented character are expelled by severe civic penalties when bad nature drives them toward godlessness, insolence, and injustice.
textual_basis: At 308e-309a, the royal craft is said to hold supervisory power over lawful educators and nurturers, not permitting practice unless it produces a fitting character for the royal mixture and commanding only those things to be taught. The same sentence says that people unable to share courageous and moderate character, and other character tending toward virtue, are pushed by bad nature toward godlessness, insolence, and injustice and are cast out by deaths, exiles, and the greatest dishonors.
limits: This records the educational limit and severe exclusion for virtue-resistant characters. It does not evaluate the justice of those penalties or treat the passage as a general legal code.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ignorance, baseness, servile class, noble, education, mixture, courage, orderliness
observation: After excluding the violently bad, the Stranger assigns people rolling in great ignorance and baseness to a servile class, while the remaining noble natures are said to be capable, through education, of receiving an artful mixture with one another.
textual_basis: At 309a-309b, the Stranger says those rolling in ignorance and much baseness are yoked under the servile class. He then turns to the remaining people, whose natures are capable of being established toward nobility when they meet with education and of receiving an artful mixture with one another, before distinguishing the courage-tending and orderly-tending groups.
limits: This records the sorting of human material before the weaving image is fully developed. It does not continue into the later account of divine and human bonds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - συνδεῖν
  - συμπλέκειν
english_gloss: courage, warp-like, firm character, orderly, soft, weft thread, bind, interweave
observation: "The weaving paradigm is mapped onto the two opposed character types: the courage-tending nature is treated as warp-like and firm, while the orderly-tending nature is treated as a soft weft-like thread, and the royal task is to bind and interweave the opposed strands."
textual_basis: At 309b, the Stranger says that among educable noble natures, those tending more toward courage are regarded as warp-like because of their firm character, while those tending toward the orderly are used as a soft, wool-like thread according to the image. These opposed strands are then said to be bound and interwoven in a certain way.
limits: This records the explicit mapping of the weaving image onto character types. It does not yet explain the divine and human bonds by which the binding is performed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The first bond of the royal interweaving is called divine: it joins the soul's ever-born part according to kinship, and the Stranger identifies it as firm true opinion about fine, just, good things and their opposites when it arises in souls."
textual_basis: At 309c, the Stranger first distinguishes a divine bond for the ever-born part of the soul from later human bonds for the living-born part. When Young Socrates asks for clarification, the Stranger says that true opinion with confirmation about fine, just, and good things and their opposites is divine when it comes to be in souls.
limits: This records the cognitive item named as the divine bond. It does not explain the subsequent human bonds or the effects of receiving this opinion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: "The Stranger makes the ability to implant the just-described condition in properly educated recipients a naming criterion: only the politician and good lawgiver should be able to do this by the royal art, and anyone unable to do it should not receive the names now being sought."
textual_basis: At 309d, the Stranger asks whether they know that only the politician and good lawgiver ought to be able, through the royal music, to implant this very thing in those who have rightly shared in education. He then says that anyone unable to do such a thing should never be addressed by the names now under inquiry, and Young Socrates strongly agrees.
limits: This records the title-disqualifying criterion stated in the marker. The marker points back to the preceding account for what is implanted and breaks off before the next effect on courageous and orderly souls.
```

```yaml
english_gloss: truth, just things, orderly nature, moderate, prudent, interweaving, bond, bad, good
observation: At 309e, participation in truth, just things, and the named opinions is said to civilize one character type and to make the orderly nature moderate and prudent. The same marker denies that this bond is stable among bad characters or between good and bad characters.
textual_basis: The cited marker first links participation in truth and just things with taming and civic sharing, then says the orderly nature becomes moderate and prudent by sharing in the same opinions and receives a reproachful reputation for folly without them. It closes by saying this interweaving and bond should never be called lasting for bad characters with each other or for good characters with bad characters.
limits: This records the eligibility condition for the bond as stated in the marker. It does not identify the previous marker's character type beyond what is visible in the cited span, and it does not infer a general theory of moral education.

```

```yaml
  - εὐγενέσι
  - νόμων
  - τέχνῃ φάρμακον
  - θειότερον
  - σύνδεσμον
  - ἀρετῆς μερῶν
  - ἀνομοίων
  - ἐναντία
english_gloss: noble, laws, craft remedy, more divine, bond, parts of virtue, unlike, opposites
observation: At 310a, the Stranger limits the craft remedy to noble characters raised according to nature under laws, and calls this the more divine bond between unlike parts of virtue that tend toward opposites.
textual_basis: The marker says the bond is implanted only in characters noble from the start and naturally reared through laws, and that the remedy belongs to craft. It then identifies this as the more divine bond joining unlike parts of virtue that move toward opposite directions.
limits: This record stays with the divine bond and its stated recipients. It does not specify the remaining human bonds except to note that the marker says they become easy once this divine bond is present.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - προσομοίους
  - ἀνομοίους
english_gloss: intermarriages, child associations, givings in marriage, marriages, wealth, powers, lineages, ease, similar, unlike
observation: At 310b-310c, the remaining human bonds are identified as intermarriages, child associations, and private marriage arrangements. The passage says many people bind these badly for child-generation, dismisses pursuit of wealth and power as not worth extended blame, and faults lineage managers for seeking immediate ease by welcoming similar people and refusing unlike people.
textual_basis: The cited span names the human bonds and says many do not bind them correctly with a view to producing children. It then sets aside wealth and power pursuits, shifts criticism to those caring for lineages, and says they act from no correct account because they pursue present ease by embracing those like themselves and not loving unlike people.
limits: This records the marriage-bond diagnosis in the cited span. It does not yet include the later account of what happens to unmixed character lines across generations.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - νωθεστέρα
  - ἀναπηροῦσθαι
  - γενέσεσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger explains why unmixed character lines are defective across generations: courage without moderation first flourishes in strength but ends in madness, while reserve without daring courage becomes too sluggish and finally disabled."
textual_basis: The argument says unmixed courage over many generations first reaches strength and then flowers into madness. It then says a soul full of reserve and unmixed with daring courage becomes more sluggish than the due measure and ends by being disabled.
limits: The preceding same-type marriage diagnosis is covered by the previous accepted observation, and the royal weaving remedy that begins at the end of the span is covered by the following accepted observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: bonds, fine and good things, one opinion, royal interweaving, moderate, courageous, shared opinions, honors, dishonors, fabric, offices
observation: At 310e-311a, the royal interweaving is described as keeping moderate and courageous characters from separating by binding them through one opinion about fine and good things, shared opinions, honors, dishonors, reputations, and pledged exchanges. The result is a smooth, well-woven fabric, and city offices are to be entrusted to these types jointly.
textual_basis: The cited span says the relevant bonds are easy once both groups have one opinion about fine and good things. It then identifies the whole work of royal interweaving as never allowing moderate characters to stand apart from courageous ones, but interlacing them through shared opinions, honors, dishonors, reputations, and exchanges, before gathering a smooth fabric and entrusting offices to them in common.
limits: This records the bonding instruments named in the span and the joint assignment of offices. It does not yet specify the later distinction between one ruler with both traits and multiple rulers mixed from each type.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ἀνδρεῖά
  - ἰταμὸν
english_gloss: ruler, overseer, mixing together, moderate rulers, cautious, just, preservative, sharpness, courageous things, bold
observation: "At 311a-311b, the Stranger gives an office-assignment rule: where one ruler is needed, choose someone with both traits; where several are needed, mix members of each type. The justification is that moderate rulers are cautious, just, and preservative but lack sharp practical boldness, while courageous rulers are weaker in justice and caution but excel in boldness in action; both are needed for city affairs to go well privately and publicly."
textual_basis: The cited span says a single overseer should have both traits and a plural ruling group should mix a share of each type. It then contrasts the strengths and lacks of moderate rulers and courageous rulers, and concludes that city affairs cannot turn out well without both being present.
limits: This records the assignment principle and the paired trait comparison. It does not describe a specific constitutional office or institutional procedure.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: end of the fabric, political action, courageous, moderate, concord, friendship, royal art, slaves, free people, weave, rules, supervises
observation: At 311b-311c, the endpoint of political action is called the straight-woven fabric of courageous and moderate character. Royal art gathers their common life in concord and friendship, makes the best and most magnificent fabric, clothes all city members, slave and free, holds them together in the weave, and rules and supervises the city without omitting what belongs to a happy city.
textual_basis: The cited span names the final fabric of political action as the character of courageous and moderate people woven together. It then assigns to royal art the gathering of their common life by concord and friendship, the completion of the best fabric, the clothing of all slaves and free people in cities, and the holding, ruling, and supervising of the city through this weave.
limits: This records the final weaving formulation and its scope. It does not turn the weaving language into a hidden doctrine, and it does not infer a detailed institutional constitution from the metaphor.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The text at 273e says that the end of all has been stated, and what is sufficient for the demonstration of the king follows from what was touched upon before. This is an explicit boundary marker between mythic narrative and argumentative purpose.
```

```yaml
observation: At 273d-273e the arranging god intervenes as helmsman to rescue the cosmos from dissolution into the infinite sea of unlikeness, restoring and correcting what had deteriorated, making it immortal and ageless.
textual_basis: The text describes the god who arranged the cosmos seeing it in distress, taking up the helm again, reversing what had decayed, and ordering it anew to make it immortal and ageless. The helmsman imagery frames divine agency as a corrective intervention within the mythic narrative.
limits: The observation records a mythic episode of divine rescue; it does not interpret the helmsman image allegorically or connect it to the dialogue's political argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - αὐτοκράτορα
  - φύειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 273e-274a the cosmic reversal is described as reversing biological processes: animals that were nearly destroyed grow large again, earth-born bodies with grey hair die back into the earth, and generation shifts from external reproduction to self-generation by the parts of the cosmos, imitating the self-governance of the whole."
textual_basis: The text at 273e states that aging reversed and contrary things were produced; at 274a it says all things changed following the cosmos's condition, and the imitation of conception, birth, and nourishment followed by necessity, with animals no longer generated through others but through themselves, just as the cosmos was appointed self-governing in its course so too the parts.
limits: The observation records mythic narrative content about reversed natural order; it does not assess the coherence of the biology described or its relation to any philosophical argument outside this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ἀγελαιοκομικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 276a the Stranger proposes reapplying the same division cuts used earlier in the dialogue (pedestian versus wingless, unmixed versus hornless) to separate the current kingship from the age-of-Cronus herd-keeping, explicitly saying the division proceeds "in the same way as before."
textual_basis: The Stranger says the division would proceed according to the same distinctions by which they previously divided herd-tending, and by using those same cuts they would encompass both the present and the Cronus-era kingship.
limits: The observation notes the reuse of prior cuts but does not assess whether the reused division is logically valid or whether the Cronus-era contrast is a genuine definitional distinction.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀγελαιοκομικῆς
  - θρεπτικοῦ
  - ἐπιμέλεια
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 276b the Stranger explains that using the name "herd-keeping" instead of "rearing" would have prevented the earlier dispute in which no art was judged worthy of the rearing label, because the new term would immediately be acknowledged as a form of care.
textual_basis: The Stranger states that once the name "herd-keeping" was spoken, no one would dispute that it is a care, whereas before it was justly disputed that no art among us deserved the rearing label, since many others would claim it before any king.
limits: The observation records the renaming strategy and its stated rationale; it does not evaluate whether the new label is more accurate or whether the prior dispute was justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 276c the Stranger retrospectively identifies that even near the end of the earlier division, a significant error was made: after identifying a rearing art for the two-footed herd, they should not have immediately called it royal and political, because the name still needed further refinement."
textual_basis: The Stranger asks whether they realize that near the very end many mistakes were made, and specifies that even if they had identified a rearing art for the two-footed herd, they should not have straightway proclaimed it royal and political as completed; the name needed to be reworked first.
limits: The observation records the Stranger's retrospective correction of a premature labeling move; it does not assess whether the correction is itself sufficient or what the correct final label should be.
review_status: accepted
```
